# Financial Resilience Evaluation: From Conditional Expectations to Dynamic Convex Risk Measures

**arXiv ID**: 2606.30070v1
**Authors**: Matteo Ferrari, Roger J. A. Laeven, Emanuela Rosazza Gianin, Marco Zullino
**Published**: 2026-06-29
**Categories**: q-fin.MF, math.PR
**Comments**: 55 pages
**HTML URL**: https://arxiv.org/html/2606.30070v1

## Abstract

Financial resilience concerns the rate at which a position recovers, or further deteriorates, in response to adverse conditions. As a first step, Laeven, Ferrari, Rosazza Gianin, and Zullino (arXiv:2505.07502) introduced the resilience rate, defined as the expected instantaneous rate of (favorable) change of a price or risk-assessment process. Since this quantity captures only the conditional mean of future increments, it cannot distinguish between positions having the same expected recovery but different conditional risk profiles. We obtain a richer characterization by evaluating such increments through a genuine, possibly nonlinear, dynamic risk measure. More precisely, for an Itô process $π$ and a normalized, cash-additive dynamic risk measure $ρ$, we define the resilience evaluation by \[\mathcal D_s^ρπ_t := L^1\text{-}\lim_{\varepsilon\to0^+} \frac{1}{\varepsilon}ρ_s(π_{t+\varepsilon}-π_t), \qquad 0\leq s\leq t<T,\] whenever the limit exists. When $ρ$ is a convex dynamic risk measure induced by a BSDE with a Lipschitz or quadratic driver, we prove that this limit is well-posed and admits an explicit dual representation. It is given by the worst-case conditional expectation, over a zero-penalty class of measure changes, of an effective drift combining the drift of $π$ with the risk adjustment assigned by $ρ$ to its volatility. We further establish attainment of the optimal scenario and illustrate the scope of the construction, as well as the role of the assumptions, through examples and counterexamples.

## Abstract

Financial resilience concerns the rate at which a position recovers, or further deteriorates, in response to adverse conditions.
As a first step,[16]introduced the resilience rate, defined as the expected instantaneous rate of (favorable) change of a price or risk-assessment process.
Since this quantity captures only the conditional mean of future increments, it cannot distinguish between positions having the same expected recovery but different conditional risk profiles.
We obtain a richer characterization by evaluating such increments through a genuine, possibly nonlinear, dynamic risk measure.
More precisely, for an Itô processπ\piand a normalized, cash-additive dynamic risk measureρ\rho, we define the resilience evaluation by𝒟sρ​πt:=L1​-​limε→0+1ε​ρs​(πt+ε−πt),0≤s≤t<T,\mathcal{D}_{s}^{\rho}\pi_{t}:=L^{1}\text{-}\lim_{{\varepsilon}\to 0^{+}}\frac{1}{{\varepsilon}}\rho_{s}(\pi_{t+{\varepsilon}}-\pi_{t}),\qquad 0\leq s\leq t<T,

whenever the limit exists.
Whenρ\rhois a convex dynamic risk measure induced by a BSDE with a Lipschitz or quadratic driver, we prove that this limit is well-posed and admits an explicit dual representation.
It is given by the worst-case conditional expectation, over a zero-penalty class of measure changes, of an effective drift combining the drift ofπ\piwith the risk adjustment assigned byρ\rhoto its volatility.
We further establish attainment of the optimal scenario and illustrate the scope of the construction, as well as the role of the assumptions, through examples and counterexamples.

## 1Introduction

As risks resolve over time and information is revealed progressively, risk measurement is inherently dynamic.
The arrival of new information, and the occurrence of adverse events in particular, continuously reshape the assessment of a financial position.Dynamic risk measuresformalize this by assigning to a positionXXa process(ρt​(X))t∈[0,T](\rho_{t}(X))_{t\in[0,T]}of conditional evaluations along a filtration𝓕=(ℱt)t∈[0,T]\bm{\mathcal{F}}=(\mathcal{F}_{t})_{t\in[0,T]}, the position being acceptable at timettwheneverρt​(X)≤0\rho_{t}(X)\leq 0(see, e.g.,[5,4,14,12]).
Among these, the dynamic risk measures induced by backward stochastic differential equations (BSDEs) provide a natural and tractable class of nonlinear, time-consistent conditional valuations, whose structural properties are inherited from those of the BSDE driver (see[Peng_1997_Backward_SDE_related_g-expectation,2,9,24]).

While this literature analyzes the dynamic evolution of thelevelof risk, a complementary and largely unexplored question concerns itsrateof change once a position becomes unacceptable.
This is the perspective of financialresilience: the pace at which a position recovers, or further deteriorates, after breaching the acceptability set.
As a first step in this direction,[16]introduced theresilience rate, the instantaneous expected rate of change ofπ\piat a (possibly stopping) timet∈[0,T)t\in[0,T),˙πt|s:=L1​-​limε→0+1ε𝔼[πt+ε−πt|ℱs],\bm{\dot{}}\pi_{t|s}:=L^{1}\text{ \!-\!}\lim_{\varepsilon\to 0^{+}}\frac{1}{{\varepsilon}}\mathbb{E}\!\left[\pi_{t+\varepsilon}-\pi_{t}\,\middle|\,\mathcal{F}_{s}\right],

where the conditioning reflects the information available at an earlier times∈[0,t]s\in[0,t], andπ\piis interpreted either as a dynamic risk measure or as a price process.
The resilience rate thus measures the average instantaneous speed of recovery following an adverse event.222Empirically,[8]find that innovation and R&D are key characteristics among U.S. listed firms that are financially resilient.

The resilience rate, however, captures only the local conditional mean of the future increment.
It therefore cannot distinguish between positions that have the same expected infinitesimal recovery but very different conditional risk profiles, for instance because of different local volatility, downside exposure, or sensitivity to model misspecification.
A fuller characterization of financial resilience should account for broader features of the conditional risk profile beyond its conditional mean, and hence reflect the risk attitude of the investor or regulator who evaluates it.
This calls for replacing the conditional expectation in˙​πt|s\bm{\dot{}}\pi_{t|s}with a genuine, possibly nonlinear, dynamic risk measure.

We formalize this idea through a nested, two-layer construction.
The “inner” layer is a real-valued price/risk processπ\pifollowing Itô dynamics,d​πt=bt​d​t+σt⋅d​Wt,t∈[0,T],\,\mathrm{d}\pi_{t}=b_{t}\,\mathrm{d}t+{\sigma}_{t}\cdot\,\mathrm{d}W_{t},\qquad t\in[0,T],

whose local recovery or deterioration is being assessed.
The “outer” layer is a normalized, cash-additive dynamic risk measureρ=(ρs)s∈[0,T]\rho=(\rho_{s})_{s\in[0,T]}, applied to the short-time increments ofπ\pi.
We then study existence and identification of the limit𝒟sρ​πt:=L1​​-​​limε→0+1ε​ρs​(πt+ε−πt),0≤s≤t<T,\mathcal{D}_{s}^{\rho}\pi_{t}:=L^{1}\text{ \!-\!}\lim_{{\varepsilon}\to 0^{+}}\frac{1}{{\varepsilon}}\,{\rho}_{s}(\pi_{t+{\varepsilon}}-\pi_{t}),\qquad 0\leq s\leq t<T,(1.1)

which we call theresilience evaluationofπt\pi_{t}throughρs\rho_{s}.
It measures the instantaneous risk, per unit of time, of the future increment ofπ\piaround timett, as evaluated throughρ{\rho}at the present timess.
Themeanresilience rate introduced in[16]is recovered as the special caseρs(⋅)=𝔼[⋅|ℱs]\rho_{s}(\cdot)=\mathbb{E}[\,\cdot\,|\mathcal{F}_{s}], so that˙​πt|s=𝒟s𝔼​πt\bm{\dot{}}\pi_{t|s}=\mathcal{D}_{s}^{\mathbb{E}}\pi_{t}.

The resilience evaluation admits several complementary interpretations, which we discuss in Section3.2.
It is the slope at the origin of the exposure-risk curveε↦ρs​(πt+ε−πt){\varepsilon}\mapsto\rho_{s}(\pi_{t+{\varepsilon}}-\pi_{t}).
For BSDE-induced risk measures, (1.1) can also be viewed as an extension of the classical representation formula for BSDE drivers (see[15]).
Ats=ts=t, cash-additivity and normalization yield the first-order expansionρt​(πt+ε)=πt+ε​𝒟tρ​πt+oL1​(ε),as​ε→0+,{\rho}_{t}(\pi_{t+{\varepsilon}})=\pi_{t}+{\varepsilon}\,\mathcal{D}^{\rho}_{t}\pi_{t}+o_{L^{1}}({\varepsilon}),\qquad\text{as }{\varepsilon}\to 0^{+},

so that𝒟tρ​πt\mathcal{D}^{\rho}_{t}\pi_{t}is the instantaneous, risk-adjusted rate of change of the risklevelofπ\pi.
Fors<ts<t, the resilience evaluation is then the present, risk-adjusted forecast of this future instantaneous resilience.
Finally, whenρ\rhois positively homogeneous, it coincides with the present risk of the future infinitesimal rate of change ofπ\pi.

As a first validation of the definition, we compute the resilience evaluation for three benchmark cases.
For the conditional expectation,
it reduces to the mean resilience rate𝒟s𝔼​πt=𝔼​[bt|ℱs]\mathcal{D}^{\mathbb{E}}_{s}\pi_{t}=\mathbb{E}[b_{t}|\mathcal{F}_{s}], driven solely by the local driftbbofπ\pi.
For the conditional variance, the first-order contribution is purely diffusive and yieldsL1​​-​​limε→0+1ε​𝕍​(πt+ε−πt|ℱs)=𝔼​[|σt|2|ℱs],L^{1}\text{ \!-\!}\lim_{{\varepsilon}\to 0^{+}}\frac{1}{{\varepsilon}}\mathbb{V}(\pi_{t+{\varepsilon}}-\pi_{t}|\mathcal{F}_{s})=\mathbb{E}\big[|{\sigma}_{t}|^{2}\big|\mathcal{F}_{s}\big],

showing that the same infinitesimal construction can isolate the local volatility of the position.
For the entropic risk measure𝔢sγ​(X)=γ−1​ln⁡𝔼​[exp⁡(γ​X)|ℱs]\mathfrak{e}^{\gamma}_{s}(X)={\gamma}^{-1}\ln\mathbb{E}[\exp({\gamma}X)|\mathcal{F}_{s}], with risk-aversion parameterγ>0{\gamma}>0(see, e.g.,[1]), the evaluation combines both, yielding a drift term corrected by a quadratic volatility adjustment,𝒟s𝔢γ​πt=𝔼​[bt|ℱs]+γ2​𝔼​[|σt|2|ℱs].\mathcal{D}^{\mathfrak{e}^{\gamma}}_{s}\pi_{t}=\mathbb{E}[b_{t}|\mathcal{F}_{s}]+\frac{{\gamma}}{2}\mathbb{E}\big[|{\sigma}_{t}|^{2}\big|\mathcal{F}_{s}\big].

These examples already show the additional information carried by𝒟sρ​πt\mathcal{D}_{s}^{\rho}\pi_{t}: depending on the outer evaluationρ{\rho}, the same local increment can be assessed through its mean, its dispersion, or a combination of the two.

When restricted to sufficiently integrable risks, the entropic risk measure is itself induced by a BSDE, with a quadratic driverg​(z)=γ2​|z|2g(z)=\tfrac{{\gamma}}{2}|z|^{2}.
This suggests considering, as outer evaluation, the entire class of convex dynamic risk measures induced by BSDEs, whose driverggis normalized, independent of theyy-variable, and convex in thezz-variable.
We treat two regimes of growth for the driver: the Lipschitz and the quadratic case, in whichρ\rhois well-defined onℱT\mathcal{F}_{T}-measurable random variables that are square integrable or have finite exponential moments of all orders, respectively.
The key structural property we exploit is convexity: it grantsρ\rhoa dual representation as a penalized worst-case expectation (see[2]), in which the penalty term is the time integral of the convex conjugateg∗g^{\ast}of the driver:ρs​(X)=ess​maxμ∈ℬ⁡𝔼μ​[X−∫sTg∗​(r,μr)​dr|ℱs],s∈[0,T],\rho_{s}(X)=\operatorname*{ess\,max}_{\mu\in\mathcal{B}}\mathbb{E}_{\mu}\bigg[X-\int_{s}^{T}g^{\ast}(r,\mu_{r})\,\mathrm{d}r\bigg|\mathcal{F}_{s}\bigg],\qquad s\in[0,T],

where the expectation is computed with respect to the probabilityℚμ\mathbb{Q}^{\mu}generated by the stochastic exponential of∫μ⋅dW\int\mu\cdot\,\mathrm{d}W, and the dual domainℬ\mathcal{B}is a suitable subset ofBMOT\mathrm{BMO}_{T}processes.

Our main result, see Section3.5, establishes that, under the above Lipschitz or quadratic assumptions and an additional mild error-bound condition, the resilience evaluation is well-posed and admits the closed-form dual representation𝒟sρ​πt=ess​maxμ∈𝒜0⁡𝔼μ​[bt+g​(t,σt)|ℱs],0≤s≤t<T,\mathcal{D}_{s}^{\rho}\pi_{t}=\operatorname*{ess\,max}_{\mu\in\mathcal{A}^{0}}\mathbb{E}_{\mu}\big[b_{t}+g(t,{\sigma}_{t})\big|\mathcal{F}_{s}\big],\qquad 0\leq s\leq t<T,(1.2)

for almost everytt, where𝒜0\mathcal{A}^{0}is the class of processesμ∈ℬ\mu\in\mathcal{B}that take values in the zero-penalty set∂zg​(⋅,0)={q∈ℝm:g∗​(⋅,q)=0}.\partial_{z}g(\,\cdot\,,0)=\big\{q\in\mathbb{R}^{m}:\ g^{\ast}(\,\cdot\,,q)=0\big\}.

Thus the resilience evaluation is obtained as a worst conditional expectation of a localeffective driftthat combines the physical drift ofπ\piwith the risk adjustment thatρ{\rho}assigns to its volatility.
The proof is delicate and proceeds in several steps.
It starts from the dual representation ofρs​(πt+ε−πt)\rho_{s}(\pi_{t+{\varepsilon}}-\pi_{t}), rewrites the increment underℚμ\mathbb{Q}^{\mu}by Girsanov’s theorem, thus making an additional term∫tt+εσr⋅μr​dr\int_{t}^{t+{\varepsilon}}{\sigma}_{r}\cdot\mu_{r}\,\mathrm{d}rappear, and separates the penalty contribution on the short interval[t,t+ε][t,t+{\varepsilon}]from the one accumulated on[s,t][s,t].
The scaling factorε−1{\varepsilon}^{-1}then amplifies any non-vanishing penalty and forces the dual processes toward the zero-penalty set, localizing the problem to the shrinking interval[t,t+ε][t,t+{\varepsilon}]; there, the Fenchel equalityσ⋅μ−g∗​(⋅,μ)=g​(⋅,σ){\sigma}\cdot\mu-g^{\ast}(\,\cdot\,,\mu)=g(\,\cdot\,,{\sigma})converts the dual expression back into the drivergg.
Making this rigorous requires restricting the dual domain, a measurable selection, and a pasting argument before passing to theL1L^{1}-limit.
At the diagonals=ts=t, (1.2) collapses to the simple expression𝒟tρ​πt=bt+g​(t,σt)\mathcal{D}_{t}^{\rho}\pi_{t}=b_{t}+g(t,{\sigma}_{t}).

We show in Section3.5.4that the worst-case representation (1.2) is indeed attained, that is, there exists an optimal dual process achieving the essential supremum.

We then illustrate the scope of the theory with a range of examples.
First, we consider the case where the inner processπ\piis the first component of the solution(π,Zπ)(\pi,Z^{\pi})of a BSDE with drivergπg^{\pi}.
The resilience evaluation makes the interaction between the two layers explicit, the inner drivergπg^{\pi}governing the local drift ofπ\piand the outer driverggimposing a risk-adjustment to the martingale component ofπ\pi:𝒟sρ​πt=ess​maxμ∈𝒜0⁡𝔼μ​[−gπ​(t,πt,Ztπ)+g​(t,Ztπ)|ℱs].\mathcal{D}_{s}^{\rho}\pi_{t}=\operatorname*{ess\,max}_{\mu\in\mathcal{A}^{0}}\mathbb{E}_{\mu}\big[-g^{\pi}(t,\pi_{t},Z^{\pi}_{t})+g(t,Z^{\pi}_{t})\big|\mathcal{F}_{s}\big].(1.3)

As a financial illustration, we apply this to a replicating portfolio in the Black–Scholes model, whereπ\piis the price process of a European payoff andZπZ^{\pi}encodes the optimal hedging strategy.
We then specialize the outer drivergg.
Linear drivers return the conditional expectation under a change of measure, sublinear drivers yield coherent risk measures, and genuinely convex drivers produce, among others, the entropic measure, its robust counterpart obtained by adding a coherent term, and subquadratic penalties.
In each case, Theorem15delivers a closed-form expression in terms of the local characteristics(b,σ)(b,{\sigma})ofπ\piand the zero-penalty set∂zg​(⋅,0)\partial_{z}g(\,\cdot\,,0).

A few counterexamples also clarify the limits of the proposed resilience evaluation.
For dynamic risk measures not induced by BSDEs, the resilience evaluation may fail to exist: for Brownian increments, both Value-at-Risk and Expected Shortfall scale likeε\sqrt{\varepsilon}, so that the corresponding normalized limits explode asε→0+{\varepsilon}\to 0^{+}.
We also show that, if either normalization or cash-additivity forρ\rhois dropped, the limit may become ill-posed even for simple Itô dynamics.

The outline of the paper is as follows.
Section2collects the necessary preliminaries on functional spaces, dynamic risk measures, and BSDEs.
Section3introduces the resilience evaluation, develops its interpretations, and establishes the representation results together with the attainment of the optimal scenario.
Section4illustrates the theory through examples and motivates the assumptions by means of counterexamples.
Section5concludes.
The appendicesA,B,C,Dcollect the more technical proofs of several auxiliary results and of the Lipschitz case of the main theorem, which are deferred there to preserve the flow of the exposition.

## 2Preliminaries

In this section, we present the definitions of the main functional spaces used in this paper, the basic definitions of dynamic risk measures, and the required results on BSDEs and their relation with dynamic risk measures.

## 2.1Functional spaces

predictable:‖sups∈[0,t]|Ys|‖L∞<+∞},\displaystyle:=\left\{Y:{\Omega}\times[0,t]\to\mathbb{R}\ \textit{continuous
predictable }:\ \bigg\|\sup_{s\in[0,t]}|Y_{s}|\bigg\|_{L^{\infty}}<+\infty\right\},(2.3)ℋtp\displaystyle\mathcal{H}^{p}_{t}:={Z:Ω×[0,t]→ℝm​predictable:𝔼​[(∫0t|Zs|2​ds)p/2]<+∞},\displaystyle:=\text{\large$\Bigg\{$}Z:{\Omega}\times[0,t]\to\mathbb{R}^{m}\ \textit{predictable }:\ \mathbb{E}\left[\left(\int_{0}^{t}|{Z_{s}}|^{2}\,\mathrm{d}s\right)^{p/2}\right]<+\infty\text{\large$\Bigg\}$},(2.4)BMOt\displaystyle\mathrm{BMO}_{t}:={Z:Ω×[0,t]→ℝmpredictable:∥sups∈[0,t]𝔼[∫st|Zr|2dr|ℱs]∥L∞<+∞}.\displaystyle:=\Bigg\{Z:{\Omega}\times[0,t]\to\mathbb{R}^{m}\ \textit{predictable }:\ \bigg\|\sup_{s\in[0,t]}\mathbb{E}\left[\left.\int_{s}^{t}|{Z_{r}}|^{2}\,\mathrm{d}r\right|\mathcal{F}_{s}\right]\bigg\|_{L^{\infty}}<+\infty\Bigg\}.(2.5)

With slight abuse of notation, we use the same symbols for the normed spaces obtained as quotients of the above under the equivalence relation induced by their respective natural (semi)norms.
With this convention, a process in𝒮tp\mathcal{S}^{p}_{t},𝒮texp\mathcal{S}^{\mathrm{exp}}_{t}or𝒮t∞\mathcal{S}^{\infty}_{t}is unique up to indistinguishability (and, for each equivalence class, we select the continuous representative), while processes inℋtp\mathcal{H}^{p}_{t},BMOt\mathrm{BMO}_{t}are unique up to a set of nullℙ⊗ℓ1\mathbb{P}\otimes\ell_{1}-measure (and, for each equivalence class, we select the appropriately measurable representative).

For any continuous local martingaleMMwithM0=0M_{0}=0, we denote its stochastic exponential byℰ​(M):=exp⁡(M−12​⟨M⟩){\mathcal{E}(M):=\exp\big(M-\frac{1}{2}\langle M\rangle\big)}, where⟨M⟩\langle M\rangleis its quadratic variation process.
By[Kazamaki_1994_Continuous_exponential_martingales_BMO, Theorem 1.2],ℰ​(M)\mathcal{E}(M)is a strictly positive local martingale and hence a supermartingale.
Ifμ∈BMOT\mu\in\mathrm{BMO}_{T}, then we denote its stochastic integral by(μ∙W):=∫0⋅μ⋅dW(\mu\mathbin{\vbox{\hbox{\tiny$\bullet$}}}W):=\int_{0}^{\,\cdot}\mu\cdot\,\mathrm{d}Wand the respective stochastic exponential byℰμ:=ℰ​(μ∙W)\mathcal{E}^{\mu}:=\mathcal{E}(\mu\mathbin{\vbox{\hbox{\tiny$\bullet$}}}W), which is a uniformly integrable martingale by[Kazamaki_1994_Continuous_exponential_martingales_BMO, Theorem 2.3].
Moreover, by the reverse Hölder inequality in[Kazamaki_1994_Continuous_exponential_martingales_BMO, Theorem 3.1], there existq∈(1,+∞)q\in(1,+\infty)and a constantCq,μ>0C_{q,\mu}>0such that, for every[0,T][0,T]-valued stopping timeτ\tau, it holds𝔼​[(ℰTμ)q|ℱτ]≤Cq,μ​(ℰτμ)q\mathbb{E}\big[(\mathcal{E}_{T}^{\mu})^{q}\big|\mathcal{F}_{\tau}\big]\leq C_{q,\mu}(\mathcal{E}_{\tau}^{\mu})^{q}.
Eventually, we define the probability measureℚμ∼ℙ\mathbb{Q}^{\mu}\sim\mathbb{P}byd​ℚμd​ℙ:=ℰTμ=exp⁡(∫0Tμr⋅dWr−12​∫0T|μr|2​dr),\frac{\,\mathrm{d}\mathbb{Q}^{\mu}}{\,\mathrm{d}\mathbb{P}}:=\mathcal{E}^{\mu}_{T}=\exp\left(\int_{0}^{T}\mu_{r}\cdot\,\mathrm{d}W_{r}-\frac{1}{2}\int_{0}^{T}|\mu_{r}|^{2}\,\mathrm{d}r\right),(2.6)

and we denote by𝔼μ[⋅|ℱs]\mathbb{E}_{\mu}[\,\cdot\,|\mathcal{F}_{s}]the conditional expectation underℚμ\mathbb{Q}^{\mu}.
By Girsanov’s theorem (see, e.g.,[Karatzas+Shreve_1991_Brownian_motion_stochastic_calculus, Section 3.5, Theorem 5.1]) the processWtμ:=Wt−∫0tμr​drW^{\mu}_{t}:=W_{t}-\int_{0}^{t}\mu_{r}\,\mathrm{d}r, fort∈[0,T]t\in[0,T], is a Brownian motion underℚμ\mathbb{Q}^{\mu}.

Last, we recall[16, Proposition 9], a result that generalizes[15, Proposition 2.2]and that will be used extensively in the paper.

## Lemma 1.

Letψ∈LTq\psi\in L^{q}_{T}for someq≥1q\geq 1.
Forℓ1\ell_{1}-a.e.t∈[0,T)t\in[0,T)and alls∈[0,t]s\in[0,t], we have:ψt=Lq​​-​​limε→0+1ε​∫tt+εψr​dr,𝔼​[ψt|ℱs]=Lq​​-​​limε→0+𝔼​[1ε​∫tt+εψr​dr|ℱs].\displaystyle\psi_{t}=L^{q}\text{ \!-\!}\lim_{\varepsilon\to 0^{+}}\frac{1}{\varepsilon}\int_{t}^{t+\varepsilon}\psi_{r}\,\mathrm{d}r,\qquad\mathbb{E}[\psi_{t}|\mathcal{F}_{s}]=L^{q}\text{ \!-\!}\lim_{\varepsilon\to 0^{+}}\mathbb{E}\left[\left.\frac{1}{\varepsilon}\int_{t}^{t+\varepsilon}\psi_{r}\,\mathrm{d}r\right|\mathcal{F}_{s}\right].(2.7)

## 2.2Dynamic risk measures

We set the basic definition of dynamic risk measure, adopting the actuarial sign convention and interpreting it as risk evaluation of an asset through time; see[2,14].

## Definition 2(Dynamic risk measure).

Let𝒳T\mathcal{X}_{T}be a vector subspace ofL1​(ℱT)L^{1}(\mathcal{F}_{T})containing constants, and define𝒳t:=𝒳T∩L1​(ℱt)\mathcal{X}_{t}:=\mathcal{X}_{T}\cap L^{1}(\mathcal{F}_{t})fort∈[0,T)t\in[0,T).
A dynamic risk measure on𝒳T\mathcal{X}_{T}is a familyρ=(ρt)t∈[0,T]\rho=(\rho_{t})_{t\in[0,T]}such thatρt:𝒳T→𝒳t{\rho}_{t}:\mathcal{X}_{T}\to\mathcal{X}_{t}for anyt∈[0,T]t\in[0,T], and further satisfies the monotonicity property:
For anyX,Y∈𝒳TX,Y\in\mathcal{X}_{T}, ifX≤YX\leq Y, thenρt​(X)≤ρt​(Y)\rho_{t}(X)\leq\rho_{t}(Y)for anyt∈[0,T]t\in[0,T].

A static risk measure is defined as the evaluation at time0of a dynamic risk measure; see[6,12,13,3].

Let us now fix𝒳T\mathcal{X}_{T}as above.
We present a non-exhaustive list of well-known axioms for a dynamic risk measureρ{\rho}on𝒳T\mathcal{X}_{T}.
- 1.

Normalization:ρt​(0)=0\rho_{t}(0)=0for anyt∈[0,T]t\in[0,T].
- 2.

Cash-additivity: ForX∈𝒳TX\in\mathcal{X}_{T}, andt∈[0,T]t\in[0,T], ifh∈𝒳th\in\mathcal{X}_{t}, thenρt​(X+h)=ρt​(X)+h\rho_{t}(X+h)=\rho_{t}(X)+h.
- 3.

Positive homogeneity:ρt​(α​X)=α​ρt​(X)\rho_{t}(\alpha X)=\alpha\rho_{t}(X)forX∈𝒳TX\in\mathcal{X}_{T},α>0{\alpha}>0, andt∈[0,T]t\in[0,T].
- 4.

Convexity:ρt​(λ​X+(1−λ)​Y)≤λ​ρt​(X)+(1−λ)​ρt​(Y)\rho_{t}\big(\lambda X+(1-\lambda)Y\big)\leq\lambda\rho_{t}(X)+(1-\lambda)\rho_{t}(Y), forX,Y∈𝒳TX,Y\in\mathcal{X}_{T},λ∈[0,1]\lambda\in[0,1], andt∈[0,T]t\in[0,T].
- 5.

Coherence: cash-additivity + convexity + positive homogeneity.

For further details on the financial interpretation of the properties listed above, interested readers are referred to[12,13,2,24].
We here briefly recall that positive homogeneity implies normalization, hence a coherent risk measure is always normalized.

Ifρ\rhois a dynamic risk measure on𝒳T\mathcal{X}_{T}, andX∈𝒳TX\in\mathcal{X}_{T}, we will often work with the naturally defined𝓕\bm{\mathcal{F}}-adapted stochastic processρ​(X):Ω×[0,T]∋(ω,t)↦ρt​(X)​(ω)∈ℝ{\rho(X):{\Omega}\times[0,T]\ni({\omega},t)\mapsto\rho_{t}(X)({\omega})\in\mathbb{R}}.
This notation highlights the connection to BSDEs, which is going to be examined in the next subsection.

## 2.3Backward stochastic differential equations

We now provide the main results on BSDEs that we will use in this paper.

## Definition 3.

We call driver a𝒫⊗ℬ​(ℝ)⊗ℬ​(ℝm)\mathcal{P}\otimes\mathscr{B}(\mathbb{R})\otimes\mathscr{B}(\mathbb{R}^{m})-measurable functiong:Ω×[0,T]×ℝ×ℝm∋(ω,t,y,z)↦g​(ω,t,y,z)∈ℝ.g:{\Omega}\times[0,T]\times\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{R}^{m}\ni({\omega},t,y,z)\mapsto g({\omega},t,y,z)\in\mathbb{R}.

Ifggis a driver,T′∈(0,T]T^{\prime}\in(0,T], andX:Ω→ℝX:{\Omega}\to\mathbb{R}isℱT′\mathcal{F}_{T^{\prime}}-measurable, then a solution to the BSDE with parameters(g,T′,X)(g,T^{\prime},X)is a couple(Y,Z)∈𝒮T′2×ℋT′2(Y,Z)\in\mathcal{S}_{T^{\prime}}^{2}\times\mathcal{H}_{T^{\prime}}^{2}such that, for anyt∈[0,T′]t\in[0,T^{\prime}]:Yt=X+∫tT′g​(s,Ys,Zs)​ds−∫tT′Zs⋅dWs.Y_{t}=X+\int_{t}^{T^{\prime}}g(s,Y_{s},Z_{s})\,\mathrm{d}s-\int_{t}^{T^{\prime}}Z_{s}\cdot\,\mathrm{d}W_{s}.(2.8)

We recall some theorems of existence, uniqueness and regularity for solutions to BSDEs.
- (𝐋)\mathbf{(L)}

Let a driverggsatisfy the following assumptions.
There existp∈[2,+∞)p\in[2,+\infty)andL≥0L\geq 0such thatg​(⋅,0,0)∈LTpg(\,\cdot\,,0,0)\in L^{p}_{T}andℙ⊗ℓ1\mathbb{P}\otimes\ell_{1}-a.e., for all(y,z),(y′,z′)∈ℝ×ℝm(y,z),(y^{\prime},z^{\prime})\in\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{R}^{m}:|g​(⋅,y,z)−g​(⋅,y′,z′)|≤L​(|y−y′|+|z−z′|),|g(\,\cdot\,,y,z)-g(\,\cdot\,,y^{\prime},z^{\prime})|\leq L\big(|y-y^{\prime}|+|z-z^{\prime}|\big),

Then, for everyX∈Lp​(ℱT)X\in L^{p}(\mathcal{F}_{T})there exists a unique solution(Y,Z)(Y,Z)to the BSDE (2.8) with parameters(g,T,X)(g,T,X).
Moreover,(Y,Z)∈𝒮Tp×ℋTp(Y,Z)\in\mathcal{S}^{p}_{T}\times\mathcal{H}^{p}_{T}.
See[Pardoux+Peng_1990_Adapted_solution_backward_stochastic_differential_equation]and[Zhang_2017_Backward_stochastic_differential_equations, Theorems 4.2.1, 4.3.1].
- (𝐐)\mathbf{(Q)}

Let a driverggsatisfy the following assumptions.
There exist constantsβ,γ,K>0\beta,\gamma,K>0and a predictable processα:Ω×[0,T]→[0,+∞)\alpha:{\Omega}\times[0,T]\to[0,+\infty)such that∫0Tα​dℓ1∈L∞\int_{0}^{T}{\alpha}\,\mathrm{d}\ell_{1}\in L^{\infty}and,ℙ⊗ℓ1\mathbb{P}\otimes\ell_{1}-a.e., the following conditions hold for all(y,z),(y′,z′)∈ℝ×ℝm(y,z),(y^{\prime},z^{\prime})\in\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{R}^{m}:|g​(⋅,y,z)|≤α+β​|y|+γ​|z|2,\displaystyle|g(\,\cdot\,,y,z)|\leq\alpha+\beta|y|+\gamma|z|^{2},(2.9)|g​(⋅,y,z)−g​(⋅,y′,z′)|≤K​[|y−y′|+(1+|y|+|y′|+|z|+|z′|)​|z−z′|].\displaystyle|g(\,\cdot\,,y,z)-g(\,\cdot\,,y^{\prime},z^{\prime})|\leq K\big[|y-y^{\prime}|+(1+|y|+|y^{\prime}|+|z|+|z^{\prime}|)|z-z^{\prime}|\big].(2.10)

Then, for everyX∈L∞​(ℱT)X\in L^{\infty}(\mathcal{F}_{T}), there exists a unique solution(Y,Z)(Y,Z)to the BSDE (2.8) with parameters(g,T,X)(g,T,X).
Moreover,(Y,Z)∈𝒮T∞×BMOT(Y,Z)\in\mathcal{S}^{\infty}_{T}\times\mathrm{BMO}_{T}.
Existence follows from[Briand+Hu_2008_Quadratic_BSDEs_convex_generators_unbounded_terminal_conditions, Lemma 2].
Uniqueness and regularities follow by adapting the standard a priori estimates and linearisation arguments in[Zhang_2017_Backward_stochastic_differential_equations, Theorems 7.2.1, 7.3.1], see also[Kobylanski_2000_Backward_stochastic_differential_equations_partial_differential_equations_quadratic_growth,19].
- (𝐄)\mathbf{(E)}

Let a driverggsatisfy the following assumptions.
There exist constantsβ,γ>0\beta,\gamma>0, and a predictable processα:Ω×[0,T]→[0,+∞)\alpha:{\Omega}\times[0,T]\to[0,+\infty)such that∫0Tα​dℓ1∈Lexp\int_{0}^{T}{\alpha}\,\mathrm{d}\ell_{1}\in L^{\mathrm{exp}}and,ℙ⊗ℓ1\mathbb{P}\otimes\ell_{1}-a.e., the following conditions hold for ally,y′∈ℝy,y^{\prime}\in\mathbb{R},z∈ℝmz\in\mathbb{R}^{m}:ℝm∋z′↦g​(⋅,y,z′)∈ℝ​is convex,\displaystyle\mathbb{R}^{m}\ni z^{\prime}\mapsto g(\,\cdot\,,y,z^{\prime})\in\mathbb{R}\text{ is convex},(2.11)|g​(⋅,y,z)−g​(⋅,y′,z)|≤β​|y−y′|,\displaystyle|g(\,\cdot\,,y,z)-g(\,\cdot\,,y^{\prime},z)|\leq\beta|y-y^{\prime}|,(2.12)|g​(⋅,y,z)|≤α+β​|y|+γ​|z|2.\displaystyle|g(\,\cdot\,,y,z)|\leq\alpha+\beta|y|+\gamma|z|^{2}.(2.13)

Then, for everyX∈Lexp​(ℱT)X\in L^{\mathrm{exp}}(\mathcal{F}_{T}), there exists a unique solution(Y,Z)(Y,Z)to the BSDE
 (2.8) with parameters(g,T,X)(g,T,X)such thatY∈𝒮TexpY\in\mathcal{S}^{\mathrm{exp}}_{T}andZ∈ℋTpZ\in\mathcal{H}^{p}_{T}for everyp≥1p\geq 1.
See[Briand+Hu_2008_Quadratic_BSDEs_convex_generators_unbounded_terminal_conditions, Corollary 6].

## Remark 4.

Consider a driverggsatisfying one of the three conditions(𝐋)\mathbf{(L)},(𝐐)\mathbf{(Q)},(𝐄)\mathbf{(E)}above, and set𝒳T=Lp​(ℱT)\mathcal{X}_{T}=L^{p}(\mathcal{F}_{T}),𝒳T=L∞​(ℱT)\mathcal{X}_{T}=L^{\infty}(\mathcal{F}_{T}), or𝒳T=Lexp​(ℱT)\mathcal{X}_{T}=L^{\mathrm{exp}}(\mathcal{F}_{T}), respectively.
Then the first components of the solutions to the family of BSDEs with parameters(g,T,X)(g,T,X), asXXvaries in𝒳T\mathcal{X}_{T}, naturally define a conditional non-linear expectation, see[Peng_1997_Backward_SDE_related_g-expectation,24,Kratschmer+Ladkau+Laeven+Schoenmakers+Stadje_2018_Optimal_stopping_under_uncertainty_drift_jump_intensity], or, according to Definition2, a dynamic risk measure on𝒳T\mathcal{X}_{T}.
Indeed, ifYXY^{X}denotes the first component of the solution to the BSDE with parameters(g,T,X)(g,T,X), then the functionρt:𝒳T∋X↦YtX∈𝒳t{\rho_{t}:\mathcal{X}_{T}\ni X\mapsto Y^{X}_{t}\in\mathcal{X}_{t}}is well-defined for anyt∈[0,T]t\in[0,T], and the comparison theorem (see[Zhang_2017_Backward_stochastic_differential_equations, Theorems 4.4.1, 7.3.1]and[Briand+Hu_2008_Quadratic_BSDEs_convex_generators_unbounded_terminal_conditions, Theorem 5]) guarantees the monotonicity property.
We say that the dynamic risk measureρ\rhoconstructed above is induced (on𝒳T\mathcal{X}_{T}) by the (family of BSDEs with) drivergg.
Conversely, we say that a given dynamic risk measureρ\rhois BSDE-induced if there exists a driverggsatisfying(𝐋)\mathbf{(L)},(𝐐)\mathbf{(Q)}, or(𝐄)\mathbf{(E)},
such thatρ\rhois induced bygg.
There are sufficient conditions under which dynamic
risk measures are BSDE-induced,
see[22,23,24,Laeven+RosazzaGianin+Zullino_2025_Geometric_bsdes].

As is well-known, the properties of BSDE-induced dynamic risk measures are mainly dictated by the corresponding drivers.
In the following list, we make explicit the relation between the driverggand the induced dynamic risk measure (see, for instance,[2,15,17,24]).
Letggbe a driver satisfying either(𝐋)\mathbf{(L)},(𝐐)\mathbf{(Q)}, or(𝐄)\mathbf{(E)}.
Letρ\rhodenote the dynamic risk measure induced bygg, as explained in Remark4.
Then the following properties hold.
- 1.

Normalization:ρ\rhois normalized if and only ifg​(⋅,0,0)=0g(\,\cdot\,,0,0)=0.
- 2.

Cash-additivity:ρ\rhois cash-additive ifggis independent ofyy, namely,ℙ⊗ℓ1\mathbb{P}\otimes\ell_{1}-a.e., we haveg​(⋅,y,z)=g​(⋅,0,z)g(\,\cdot\,,y,z)=g(\,\cdot\,,0,z)for all(y,z)∈ℝ×ℝm(y,z)\in\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{R}^{m}.
In this case, we identifyggwith a driver defined onΩ×[0,T]×ℝm{\Omega}\times[0,T]\times\mathbb{R}^{m}.
- 3.

Positive homogeneity:
Ifggisℙ⊗ℓ1\mathbb{P}\otimes\ell_{1}-a.e. positively homogeneous in(y,z)(y,z),
thenρ\rhois positively homogeneous.
- 4.

Convexity:
Ifggisℙ⊗ℓ1\mathbb{P}\otimes\ell_{1}-a.e. convex in(y,z)(y,z), thenρ\rhois convex.
- 5.

Coherence:
Ifggis independent ofyyand isℙ⊗ℓ1\mathbb{P}\otimes\ell_{1}-a.e. convex and positively homogeneous inzz, thenρ\rhois coherent.

The converse implications, namely the derivation of structural properties of the driver from the corresponding properties of the induced risk measure, hold under additional assumptions ensuring the local representation of the driver.
We refer to[15]in the Lipschitz case and[20,Zheng+Li_2018_Representation_theorem_generators_quadratic_BSDEs]in the quadratic case.

## 3Resilience Evaluation

This section introduces the resilience evaluation and develops its main representations.
We first give the definition, relate it to the resilience rate of[16], and discuss its principal interpretations.
We then examine the conditional variance and the entropic risk measure as preliminary benchmarks.
Finally, we establish the representation theorem for BSDE-induced convex risk measures, present its coherent specialization, and study the attainment of the resulting essential supremum.

## 3.1Definition and conditional-expectation benchmark

The construction is formulated for a general dynamic risk measure and a continuous Itô process.
Later on, both objects will be specialized in several directions.

Assume that𝒳T\mathcal{X}_{T}is a vector subspace ofL1​(ℱT)L^{1}(\mathcal{F}_{T})containing the constants, and set𝒳t:=𝒳T∩L1​(ℱt)\mathcal{X}_{t}:=\mathcal{X}_{T}\cap L^{1}(\mathcal{F}_{t})for allt∈[0,T]t\in[0,T].
Fix a normalized and cash-additive dynamic risk measureρ\rhoon𝒳T\mathcal{X}_{T}.
Letπ=(πt)t∈[0,T]\pi=(\pi_{t})_{t\in[0,T]}be a real-valued Itô process with dynamicsd​πt=bt​d​t+σt⋅d​Wt,t∈[0,T],\,\mathrm{d}\pi_{t}=b_{t}\,\mathrm{d}t+{\sigma}_{t}\cdot\,\mathrm{d}W_{t},\qquad t\in[0,T],(3.1)

whereb∈LT1b\in L^{1}_{T}is𝓕\bm{\mathcal{F}}-adapted andσ∈ℋT2{\sigma}\in\mathcal{H}^{2}_{T}.
Further assume that for anyt∈[0,T)t\in[0,T)existsh∈(0,T−t]h\in(0,T-t]suh that the incrementΔε​πt:=πt+ε−πt\Delta_{\varepsilon}\pi_{t}:=\pi_{t+{\varepsilon}}-\pi_{t}is in𝒳T\mathcal{X}_{T}for allε∈(0,h]{\varepsilon}\in(0,h].

## Definition 5.

Let0≤s≤t<T0\leq s\leq t<T.
Whenever the following limit exists inL1​(ℱs)L^{1}(\mathcal{F}_{s}), we define theresilience evaluationofπt\pi_{t}throughρs{\rho}_{s}by𝒟sρ​πt:=L1​​-​​limε→0+1ε​ρs​(πt+ε−πt).\mathcal{D}^{\rho}_{s}\pi_{t}:=L^{1}\text{ \!-\!}\lim_{\varepsilon\to 0^{+}}\frac{1}{{\varepsilon}}{\rho}_{s}(\pi_{t+{\varepsilon}}-\pi_{t}).

We first illustrate the definition through the conditional expectation.
This elementary benchmark also identifies the connection between the resilience evaluation𝒟ρ​π\mathcal{D}^{\rho}\piand the resilience rate˙​π\bm{\dot{}}\piintroduced in[16].

## Example 6(Resilience rate).

Suppose that the dynamic risk measureρ{\rho}is the conditional expectation:ρs​(X)=𝔼​[X|ℱs],∀X∈L1​(ℱT),s∈[0,T].{\rho}_{s}(X)=\mathbb{E}[X|\mathcal{F}_{s}],\qquad\forall\,X\in L^{1}(\mathcal{F}_{T}),\ s\in[0,T].

Letπ\pibe as above.
Then the incrementsΔε​πt\Delta_{\varepsilon}\pi_{t}belong toL1​(ℱT)L^{1}(\mathcal{F}_{T})and the random variableρs​(Δε​πt)\rho_{s}(\Delta_{\varepsilon}\pi_{t})is well-defined inL1​(ℱs)L^{1}(\mathcal{F}_{s}), for all0≤s≤t<t+ε≤T0\leq s\leq t<t+{\varepsilon}\leq T.
By direct inspection,1ε​ρs​(πt+ε−πt)=1ε​𝔼​[∫tt+εbr​dr+∫tt+εσr⋅dWr|ℱs]=1ε​𝔼​[∫tt+εbr​dr|ℱs],\displaystyle\frac{1}{{\varepsilon}}{\rho}_{s}(\pi_{t+{\varepsilon}}-\pi_{t})=\frac{1}{{\varepsilon}}\mathbb{E}\left[\left.\int_{t}^{t+{\varepsilon}}b_{r}\,\mathrm{d}r+\int_{t}^{t+{\varepsilon}}{\sigma}_{r}\cdot\,\mathrm{d}W_{r}\right|\mathcal{F}_{s}\right]=\frac{1}{{\varepsilon}}\mathbb{E}\left[\left.\int_{t}^{t+{\varepsilon}}b_{r}\,\mathrm{d}r\right|\mathcal{F}_{s}\right],(3.2)

where we used the martingale property of the Itô integral.
In view of Lemma1, the last expression converges inL1L^{1},
asε→0+{\varepsilon}\to 0^{+}, to𝔼​[bt|ℱs]\mathbb{E}[b_{t}|\mathcal{F}_{s}]forℓ1\ell_{1}-a.e.t∈[0,T)t\in[0,T)and alls∈[0,t]s\in[0,t].
Therefore,𝒟s𝔼​πt=𝔼​[bt|ℱs],ℓ1​-a.e.​t∈[0,T),∀s∈[0,t].\mathcal{D}_{s}^{\mathbb{E}}\pi_{t}=\mathbb{E}[b_{t}|\mathcal{F}_{s}],\qquad\ell_{1}\text{-a.e. }t\in[0,T),\ \forall\,s\in[0,t].

Suppose, in addition, thatπ\piis the first component of the solution(π,Zπ)(\pi,Z^{\pi})to a BSDE with parameters(gπ,T,πT)(g^{\pi},T,\pi_{\mathchoice{\scriptscriptstyle}{\scriptscriptstyle}{\scriptscriptstyle}{\scriptscriptstyle}T}), where, for
instance,πT∈L2​(ℱT)\pi_{\mathchoice{\scriptscriptstyle}{\scriptscriptstyle}{\scriptscriptstyle}{\scriptscriptstyle}T}\in L^{2}(\mathcal{F}_{T})andgπg^{\pi}satisfies(𝐋)\mathbf{(L)}forp=2p=2.
Thenπ\pican be interpreted as the dynamic risk assessment of the riskπT\pi_{\mathchoice{\scriptscriptstyle}{\scriptscriptstyle}{\scriptscriptstyle}{\scriptscriptstyle}T}andb=−gπ​(⋅,π,Zπ),σ=Zπ.b=-g^{\pi}(\,\cdot\,,\pi,Z^{\pi}),\qquad{\sigma}=Z^{\pi}.(3.3)

In this case,b∈𝒮T2b\in\mathcal{S}^{2}_{T}andσ∈ℋT2{\sigma}\in\mathcal{H}^{2}_{T}, and the preceding formula
becomes𝒟s𝔼​πt=−𝔼​[gπ​(t,πt,Ztπ)|ℱs],ℓ1​-a.e.​t∈[0,T),∀s∈[0,t].\mathcal{D}_{s}^{\mathbb{E}}\pi_{t}=-\mathbb{E}\big[g^{\pi}(t,\pi_{t},Z^{\pi}_{t})\big|\mathcal{F}_{s}\big],\qquad\ell_{1}\text{-a.e. }t\in[0,T),\ \forall\,s\in[0,t].

This coincides with the conditional resilience rate˙​πt|s\bm{\dot{}}\pi_{t|s}of the riskπT\pi_{\mathchoice{\scriptscriptstyle}{\scriptscriptstyle}{\scriptscriptstyle}{\scriptscriptstyle}T}, as defined in[16].

As in the previous example, we will later specialize the space of risks𝒳T\mathcal{X}_{T}, the outer dynamic risk measureρ\rho, and the inner price/risk processπ\pito several specific settings.
In the general results of Section3.5, we shall assume thatρ\rhois induced by a family of BSDEs with drivergg, and the domain𝒳T\mathcal{X}_{T}is chosen accordingly.
Concerning the inner processπ\pi, we keep it as general as possible, imposing on its coefficientsbbandσ{\sigma}only the additional regularity needed to ensure that its increments belong to the chosen ambient space𝒳T\mathcal{X}_{T}.
Since we have specified only the dynamics forπ\pi, we may either chooseπ\pias the solution to a forward SDE with given initial conditionπ0\pi_{0}, or, as in Example6, letπ\pibe the first component of a solution(π,Zπ)(\pi,Z^{\pi})to a BSDE with parameters(gπ,T,πT)(g^{\pi},T,\pi_{\mathchoice{\scriptscriptstyle}{\scriptscriptstyle}{\scriptscriptstyle}{\scriptscriptstyle}T}).
In the latter case, the relation between the Itô coefficients and the BSDE parameters is given by (3.3).

## 3.2Interpretation

We collect here the main interpretations of the resilience evaluation introduced in Definition5.
Some of these interpretations anticipate the representation results established in Section3.5, in particular Theorem15.
- •

Risk rate of a future increment.For0≤s≤t<T0\leq s\leq t<T, the random variableρs​(πt+ε−πt)\rho_{s}(\pi_{t+{\varepsilon}}-\pi_{t})is the risk, assessed at timess, of the future exposure generated by the increment of the price/risk processπ\piover the interval[t,t+ε][t,t+{\varepsilon}].
The factorε−1{\varepsilon}^{-1}in Definition5converts this risk into a rate.
Thus𝒟sρ​πt\mathcal{D}^{\rho}_{s}\pi_{t}is the per-unit-time present risk of an infinitesimal future exposure toπ\pi.
- •

Slope of the exposure-risk curve.The idea above can be formalized as follows.
Fix0≤s≤t<T0\leq s\leq t<Tand defineφs,t:[0,T−t]∋ε↦ρs​(πt+ε−πt)∈L1​(ℱs).\varphi_{s,t}:[0,T-t]\ni{\varepsilon}\mapsto\rho_{s}(\pi_{t+{\varepsilon}}-\pi_{t})\in L^{1}(\mathcal{F}_{s}).

By normalization,φs,t​(0)=0\varphi_{s,t}(0)=0.
The mapφs,t\varphi_{s,t}gives the present risk of a future exposure toπ\pias a function of the duration of this exposure.
Therefore, wheneverφs,t\varphi_{s,t}is differentiable at0, Definition5gives𝒟sρ​πt=φs,t′​(0).\mathcal{D}^{\rho}_{s}\pi_{t}=\varphi^{\prime}_{s,t}(0).

Hence the resilience evaluation is the slope at the origin of the present risk of a future exposure, viewed as a function of the exposure duration.
- •

Extension of the representation formula for BSDE drivers.Suppose thatρ\rhois induced by a driverg:Ω×[0,T]×ℝm→ℝg:\Omega\times[0,T]\times\mathbb{R}^{m}\to\mathbb{R}.
Representation results for BSDE generators yield, for everyz∈ℝmz\in\mathbb{R}^{m}and forℓ1\ell_{1}-a.e.s∈[0,T)s\in[0,T),g​(s,z)=L1​​-​​limε→0+1ε​ρs​(z⋅(Ws+ε−Ws));g(s,z)=L^{1}\text{ \!-\!}\lim_{{\varepsilon}\to 0^{+}}\frac{1}{{\varepsilon}}\rho_{s}\big(z\cdot(W_{s+{\varepsilon}}-W_{s})\big);(3.4)

see, for instance,[15, Lemma 2.1].
Thus the driver is the infinitesimal risk rate of a rescaled Brownian increment.
Definition5applies the same principle to the local increment of a processπ\piat a possible future timet∈[s,T)t\in[s,T).
It can therefore be interpreted as a generalization of this representation formula.
In particular, (3.4) is recovered from our definition by choosings=ts=t,σ=z{\sigma}=z, andb=0b=0; see also Remark18.
- •

First-order expansion ats=ts=t.Suppose now that0≤s=t<T0\leq s=t<T.
Normalization and cash-additivity yieldρt​(πt)=πt{\rho}_{t}(\pi_{t})=\pi_{t}andρt​(πt+ε−πt)=ρt​(πt+ε)−πt{\rho}_{t}(\pi_{t+{\varepsilon}}-\pi_{t})={\rho}_{t}(\pi_{t+{\varepsilon}})-\pi_{t}.
Therefore,𝒟tρ​πt=L1​​-​​limε→0+1ε​[ρt​(πt+ε)−ρt​(πt)],\mathcal{D}^{\rho}_{t}\pi_{t}=L^{1}\text{ \!-\!}\lim_{{\varepsilon}\to 0^{+}}\frac{1}{{\varepsilon}}\big[\rho_{t}(\pi_{t+{\varepsilon}})-\rho_{t}(\pi_{t})\big],

whenever either limit exists.
Thus, at timett, resilience evaluation coincides with the right derivative of the risk levelr↦ρt​(πr)r\mapsto\rho_{t}(\pi_{r})atr=tr=t: it is the future instantaneous change of the risklevelassociated withπ\pi, evaluated at timettthrough the risk measureρ\rho.
It follows from this formula that we can write a first-order expansion in timeρt​(πt+ε)=πt+ε​𝒟tρ​πt+o​(ε),as​ε→0+,{\rho}_{t}(\pi_{t+{\varepsilon}})=\pi_{t}+{\varepsilon}\,\mathcal{D}^{\rho}_{t}\pi_{t}+o({\varepsilon}),\qquad\text{as }{\varepsilon}\to 0^{+},

where theoois intended inL1L^{1}, or inℙ\mathbb{P}-probability.
Therefore,𝒟tρ​πt\mathcal{D}^{\rho}_{t}\pi_{t}serves also as a proxy for the computation of the instantaneous change in the risk associated withπt\pi_{t}.
- •

Present forecast of future resilience.Assume thatρ\rhois induced by a convex drivergg, then Theorem15and Remark18yield𝒟sρ​πt=ess​supμ∈𝒜0⁡𝔼μ​[𝒟tρ​πt|ℱs],ℓ1​-a.e.​t∈[0,T),∀s∈[0,t],\mathcal{D}^{\rho}_{s}\pi_{t}=\operatorname*{ess\,sup}_{\mu\in\mathcal{A}^{0}}\mathbb{E}_{\mu}\big[\mathcal{D}^{\rho}_{t}\pi_{t}\big|\mathcal{F}_{s}\big],\qquad\ell_{1}\text{-a.e. }t\in[0,T),\ \forall\,s\in[0,t],

where𝒜0\mathcal{A}^{0}is the class of predictable processesμ\musuch thatμ∈∂zg​(⋅,0)\mu\in\partial_{z}g(\,\cdot\,,0)ℙ⊗ℓ1\mathbb{P}\otimes\ell_{1}-a.e.
Recall from the previous point the interpretation of𝒟tρ​πt\mathcal{D}^{\rho}_{t}\pi_{t}as the instantaneous risk-adjusted rate of change ofπ\piat the future timett, or equivalently the right derivative atttofr↦ρt​(πr)r\mapsto\rho_{t}(\pi_{r}).
Hence𝒟sρ​πt\mathcal{D}^{\rho}_{s}\pi_{t}is the time-ssrisk-adjusted forecast of the future instantaneous resilience ofπ\piat timett, where the forecast is taken over the zero-penalty scenarios encoded by𝒜0\mathcal{A}^{0}.
- •

Risk of the rate of change under positive homogeneity.Ifρ\rhois positively homogeneous, and0≤s≤t<T0\leq s\leq t<T, then𝒟sρ​πt=L1​​-​​limε→0+ρs​(πt+ε−πtε),\mathcal{D}^{\rho}_{s}\pi_{t}=L^{1}\text{ \!-\!}\lim_{{\varepsilon}\to 0^{+}}\rho_{s}\left(\frac{\pi_{t+{\varepsilon}}-\pi_{t}}{{\varepsilon}}\right),

whenever either limit exists.
In this case, the resilience evaluation can be read as the present risk of the future infinitesimal rate of change ofπ\pi.

The next subsections prove the existence of𝒟sρ​πt\mathcal{D}^{\rho}_{s}\pi_{t}and identify it explicitly for increasingly rich classes of dynamic risk measures.

## 3.3Conditional variance

Before turning to the entropic case, we consider the conditional variance as a preparatory benchmark.
Although it is not a cash-additive risk measure, as later discussed in Remark8, its infinitesimal behavior isolates the second-order term that will reappear in the entropic resilience evaluation.

According to[10, Section 7.2], for a solution(Y,Z)(Y,Z)to a BSDE,
the absolute value ofZZis the local volatility of the conditional processYY.
Equivalently, the conditional variance of an infinitesimal increment inYYcoincides with the squared norm ofZZper unit time:𝕍​(d​Yt|ℱt)=|Zt|2​d​t.\mathbb{V}(\!\,\mathrm{d}Y_{t}|\mathcal{F}_{t})=|Z_{t}|^{2}\,\mathrm{d}t.

This informal statement stems from the following heuristic reasoning.
Lettingggbe the driver of the BSDE, we have:𝕍​(d​Yt|ℱt)\displaystyle\mathbb{V}(\!\,\mathrm{d}Y_{t}|\mathcal{F}_{t}):=𝔼​[(d​Yt−𝔼​[d​Yt|ℱt])2|ℱt]\displaystyle:=\mathbb{E}\left[\left.(\!\,\mathrm{d}Y_{t}-\mathbb{E}[\!\,\mathrm{d}Y_{t}|\mathcal{F}_{t}])^{2}\right|\mathcal{F}_{t}\right](3.5)=𝔼​[(d​Yt+g​(t,Yt,Zt)​d​t)2|ℱt]\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}\left[\left.\big(\!\,\mathrm{d}Y_{t}+g(t,Y_{t},Z_{t})\,\mathrm{d}t\big)^{2}\right|\mathcal{F}_{t}\right](3.6)=𝔼​[(Zt⋅d​Wt)2|ℱt]\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}\left[\left.(Z_{t}\cdot\,\mathrm{d}W_{t})^{2}\right|\mathcal{F}_{t}\right](3.7)=|Zt|2​d​t,\displaystyle=|Z_{t}|^{2}\,\mathrm{d}t,(3.8)

where we used𝔼​[d​Yt|ℱt]=𝔼​[−g​(t,Yt,Zt)​d​t+Zt⋅d​Wt|ℱt]=−g​(t,Yt,Zt)​d​t\mathbb{E}[\!\,\mathrm{d}Y_{t}|\mathcal{F}_{t}]=\mathbb{E}[-g(t,Y_{t},Z_{t})\,\mathrm{d}t+Z_{t}\cdot\,\mathrm{d}W_{t}|\mathcal{F}_{t}]=-g(t,Y_{t},Z_{t})\,\mathrm{d}t.

We now formalize this result, allowing at the same time the processYYto be a general Itô process.

## Theorem 7.

## Remark 8.

The conditional variance𝕍(⋅|ℱs)\mathbb{V}(\,\cdot\,|\mathcal{F}_{s})is not a dynamic cash-additive risk measure.
Although it is well-defined as a mapL∞​(ℱT)→L∞​(ℱs)L^{\infty}(\mathcal{F}_{T})\to L^{\infty}(\mathcal{F}_{s})and is normalized and convex, it is neither monotone nor cash-additive, being instead cash-invariant:𝕍​(X+m|ℱs)=𝕍​(X|ℱs)\mathbb{V}(X+m|\mathcal{F}_{s})=\mathbb{V}(X|\mathcal{F}_{s})for everym∈L∞​(ℱs)m\in L^{\infty}(\mathcal{F}_{s}).
Thus, it falls outside the class covered by our standing assumptions.
We include it nonetheless because Definition5requires only the existence of theL1L^{1}-limit.
Moreover, the variance illustrates that the limit extracts local information that is not captured by the conditional mean, namely the instantaneous conditional volatility𝔼​[|σt|2|ℱs]\mathbb{E}[|{\sigma}_{t}|^{2}|\mathcal{F}_{s}], i.e., the local quadratic variation ofπ\pi.
Eventually, the analysis of the conditional variance is preparatory for the next section, where it will reappear within our framework as the leading second-order correction of the entropic risk measure.

## 3.4Entropic risk measure

We treat the entropic risk measure separately because its explicit exponential representation allows us to derive the resilience evaluation for possibly unbounded coefficients under suitable exponential-integrability assumptions, whereas the quadratic case of Theorem15requiresb,|σ|∈LT∞b,|{\sigma}|\in L^{\infty}_{T}.

Let us define the entropic risk measure (see, e.g.,[1,18]) with (a fixed) risk aversion coefficientγ>0\gamma>0:𝔢sγ​(X)=1γ​ln⁡𝔼​[eγ​X|ℱs],s∈[0,T],\mathfrak{e}^{\gamma}_{s}(X)=\frac{1}{\gamma}\ln\mathbb{E}\left[\left.e^{\gamma X}\right|\mathcal{F}_{s}\right],\qquad s\in[0,T],

which is finiteℙ\mathbb{P}-a.s. for every real-valuedℱT\mathcal{F}_{T}-measurable random variableXXsuch that𝔼​[eγ​X]<+∞\mathbb{E}[e^{\gamma X}]<+\infty.
On this natural domain,𝔢γ\mathfrak{e}^{\gamma}is not necessarily a dynamic
risk measure in the sense of Definition2, since its domain need not be a vector space and its values need not be integrable.
This causes no difficulty here, since Definition5only requires the relevant normalized quantities to converge inL1L^{1}.
We are interested in studying the setting under which the following limit exists, and in giving an explicit expression for it:L1​​-​​limε→0+1ε​𝔢sγ​(πt+ε−πt),0≤s≤t<T.L^{1}\text{ \!-\!}\lim_{{\varepsilon}\to 0^{+}}\frac{1}{{\varepsilon}}\mathfrak{e}^{\gamma}_{s}(\pi_{t+{\varepsilon}}-\pi_{t}),\qquad 0\leq s\leq t<T.(3.21)

Here, we need to impose sufficient exponential integrability onπ\piin order for the entropic risk measure of its increments to be well-defined.
More precisely, a minimal necessary requirement for the well-posedness of𝔢sγ​(πt+ε−πt)\mathfrak{e}^{\gamma}_{s}(\pi_{t+{\varepsilon}}-\pi_{t})at allssis the following: for anyt∈[0,T)t\in[0,T)there existsδ>0\delta>0such that, for anyε∈(0,δ){\varepsilon}\in(0,\delta), the incrementΔε​πt:=πt+ε−πt\Delta_{\varepsilon}\pi_{t}:=\pi_{t+{\varepsilon}}-\pi_{t}satisfies𝔼​[exp⁡(γ​Δε​πt)]<+∞\mathbb{E}[\exp(\gamma\Delta_{\varepsilon}\pi_{t})]<+\infty.
The assumptions required in the following result are stronger
and they turn out to be sufficient for the existence of the limit in (3.21).

## Theorem 9.

Letp∈(2,+∞)p\in(2,+\infty)andγ>0{\gamma}>0.
Assume thatb,|σ|∈LTpb,|{\sigma}|\in L^{p}_{T}, and that there existsq>p/(p−2)q>p/(p-2)such that𝔼​[exp⁡(4​q​γ​∫0T|br|​dr)]<+∞,𝔼​[exp⁡(32​q2​γ2​∫0T|σr|2​dr)]<+∞.\mathbb{E}\left[\exp\Big(4q{\gamma}\int_{0}^{T}|b_{r}|\,\mathrm{d}r\Big)\right]<+\infty,\qquad\mathbb{E}\left[\exp\Big(32q^{2}{\gamma}^{2}\int_{0}^{T}|{\sigma}_{r}|^{2}\,\mathrm{d}r\Big)\right]<+\infty.(3.22)

Then, forℓ1\ell_{1}-a.e.t∈[0,T)t\in[0,T)and alls∈[0,t]s\in[0,t], the resilience evaluation ofπt\pi_{t}through𝔢sγ\mathfrak{e}^{\gamma}_{s}is well-defined inL1L^{1}and given by the formula:𝒟s𝔢γ​πt=𝔼​[bt|ℱs]+γ2​𝔼​[|σt|2|ℱs].\mathcal{D}^{\mathfrak{e}^{\gamma}}_{s}\pi_{t}=\mathbb{E}[b_{t}|\mathcal{F}_{s}]+\frac{\gamma}{2}\mathbb{E}\big[|{\sigma}_{t}|^{2}\big|\mathcal{F}_{s}\big].

A trivially sufficient assumption for (3.22) isb,|σ|∈LT∞b,|{\sigma}|\in L^{\infty}_{T}.
To prove this theorem, we first need a preliminary general result, whose proof is postponed to AppendixA.

## Proposition 10.

Let(Xn)n∈ℕ(X_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}be a sequence of random variables satisfying the following assumptions.
- (i)

There existsp>2p>2such thatXn⟶0X_{n}\longrightarrow 0inLpL^{p}, asn→+∞n\to+\infty.
- (ii)

There existsq>p/(p−2)q>p/(p-2)andK≥0K\geq 0such that𝔼​[e2​q​|Xn|]≤K\mathbb{E}\left[e^{2q|X_{n}|}\right]\leq Kfor alln∈ℕn\in\mathbb{N}.

Then for any sub-σ{\sigma}-algebra𝒢⊆ℱ\mathcal{G}\subseteq\mathcal{F}, we haveln⁡𝔼​[eXn|𝒢]=𝔼​[Xn|𝒢]+12​𝕍​(Xn|𝒢)+Rn,∀n∈ℕ,\ln\mathbb{E}\left[\left.e^{X_{n}}\right|\mathcal{G}\right]=\mathbb{E}[X_{n}|\mathcal{G}]+\frac{1}{2}\mathbb{V}(X_{n}|\mathcal{G})+R_{n},\qquad\forall\,n\in\mathbb{N},

whereRn∈L1​(𝒢)R_{n}\in L^{1}(\mathcal{G}), forn∈ℕn\in\mathbb{N}, and‖Rn‖L1=o​(‖Xn‖Lp2)\|R_{n}\|_{L^{1}}=o\left(\|X_{n}\|^{2}_{L^{p}}\right)asn→∞n\to\infty.

## Proof of Theorem9.

Fixt∈[0,T)t\in[0,T), let(εn)n∈ℕ({\varepsilon}_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}be an arbitrary positive infinitesimal sequence (such thatt+εn≤Tt+{\varepsilon}_{n}\leq Tfor alln∈ℕn\in\mathbb{N}) and denoteXn:=πt+εn−πt=∫tt+εnbr​dr+∫tt+εnσr⋅dWr,∀n∈ℕ.X_{n}:=\pi_{t+{\varepsilon}_{n}}-\pi_{t}=\int_{t}^{t+{\varepsilon}_{n}}b_{r}\,\mathrm{d}r+\int_{t}^{t+{\varepsilon}_{n}}{\sigma}_{r}\cdot\,\mathrm{d}W_{r},\qquad\forall\,n\in\mathbb{N}.

Step11.We aim to apply Proposition10to the sequence(γ​Xn)n∈ℕ({\gamma}X_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}with the exponentsp>2p>2,q>p/(p−2)q>p/(p-2)from the hypotheses of the theorem.

Sinceb,|σ|∈LTpb,|{\sigma}|\in L^{p}_{T}, we have‖Xn‖Lp\displaystyle\|X_{n}\|_{L^{p}}≤‖∫tt+εnbr​dr‖Lp+‖∫tt+εnσr⋅dWr‖Lp\displaystyle\leq\left\|\int_{t}^{t+{\varepsilon}_{n}}b_{r}\,\mathrm{d}r\right\|_{L^{p}}+\left\|\int_{t}^{t+{\varepsilon}_{n}}{\sigma}_{r}\cdot\,\mathrm{d}W_{r}\right\|_{L^{p}}(3.23)≤∫tt+εn‖br‖Lp​dr+C​‖(∫tt+εn|σr|2​dr)1/2‖Lp,\displaystyle\leq\int_{t}^{t+{\varepsilon}_{n}}\left\|b_{r}\right\|_{L^{p}}\,\mathrm{d}r+C\left\|\left(\int_{t}^{t+{\varepsilon}_{n}}|{\sigma}_{r}|^{2}\,\mathrm{d}r\right)^{1/2}\right\|_{L^{p}},(3.24)

where we resorted to the Burkholder-Davis-Gundy inequality (see, e.g.,[Revuz+Yor_1999_Continuous_martingales_Brownian_motion, Chapter IV, Theorem 4.1]).
The first term converges to0asn→∞n\to\inftyby continuity of the Lebesgue integral.
Since|σ|∈LTp|{\sigma}|\in L^{p}_{T},|σ|2|{\sigma}|^{2}isℙ\mathbb{P}-a.s. integrable in time, and so∫tt+εn|σr|2​dr⟶0\int_{t}^{t+{\varepsilon}_{n}}|{\sigma}_{r}|^{2}\,\mathrm{d}r\longrightarrow 0ℙ\mathbb{P}-a.s.

Therefore, by the triangle, and the Cauchy-Schwarz inequalities𝔼​[e2​q​|γ​Xn|]≤𝔼​[e2​q​γ​|Bn|​e2​q​γ​|Mεn|]≤(𝔼​[e4​q​γ​|Bn|])1/2​(𝔼​[e4​q​γ​|Mεn|])1/2.\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[e^{2q|{\gamma}X_{n}|}\right]\leq\mathbb{E}\left[e^{2q{\gamma}|B_{n}|}e^{2q{\gamma}|M_{{\varepsilon}_{n}}|}\right]\leq\left(\mathbb{E}\left[e^{4q{\gamma}|B_{n}|}\right]\right)^{1/2}\left(\mathbb{E}\left[e^{4q{\gamma}|M_{{\varepsilon}_{n}}|}\right]\right)^{1/2}.(3.25)

Since|Bn|≤∫0T|br|​dr|B_{n}|\leq\int_{0}^{T}|b_{r}|\,\mathrm{d}r, the first expectation is bounded uniformly innnby the first assumption in (3.22).

We can use this estimate, withλ:=4​q​γ{\lambda}:=4q{\gamma}to bound the last expectation in (3.25).

Forℓ1\ell_{1}-a.e.t∈[0,T)t\in[0,T)and alls∈[0,t]s\in[0,t]: the first term in the right-hand side converges to𝔼​[bt|ℱs]\mathbb{E}[b_{t}|\mathcal{F}_{s}]inLpL^{p}as in Example6, while the second term converges toγ​𝔼​[|σt|2|ℱs]/2{\gamma}\mathbb{E}[|{\sigma}_{t}|^{2}|\mathcal{F}_{s}]/2inL1L^{1}thanks to Theorem7.

ByStep11, there exists a deterministic sequencean→0a_{n}\to 0such that‖Rn‖L1≤an​‖Xn‖Lp2\|R_{n}\|_{L^{1}}\leq a_{n}\|X_{n}\|_{L^{p}}^{2}.

To this aim, we perform the following estimates:1εn​‖Xn‖Lp2\displaystyle\frac{1}{{\varepsilon}_{n}}\|X_{n}\|^{2}_{L^{p}}≤2εn​‖∫tt+εnbr​dr‖Lp2+2εn​‖∫tt+εnσr⋅dWr‖Lp2\displaystyle\leq\frac{2}{{\varepsilon}_{n}}\left\|\int_{t}^{t+{\varepsilon}_{n}}b_{r}\,\mathrm{d}r\right\|^{2}_{L^{p}}+\frac{2}{{\varepsilon}_{n}}\left\|\int_{t}^{t+{\varepsilon}_{n}}{\sigma}_{r}\cdot\,\mathrm{d}W_{r}\right\|^{2}_{L^{p}}(3.31)≤2εn​(𝔼​[|∫tt+εnbr​dr|p])2/p+2εn​(C​𝔼​[|∫tt+εn|σr|2​dr|p/2])2/p\displaystyle\leq\frac{2}{{\varepsilon}_{n}}\left(\mathbb{E}\left[\left|\int_{t}^{t+{\varepsilon}_{n}}b_{r}\,\mathrm{d}r\right|^{p}\right]\right)^{2/p}+\frac{2}{{\varepsilon}_{n}}\left(C\,\mathbb{E}\left[\left|\int_{t}^{t+{\varepsilon}_{n}}|{\sigma}_{r}|^{2}\,\mathrm{d}r\right|^{p/2}\right]\right)^{2/p}(3.32)≤2​εn​(1εn​∫tt+εn𝔼​[|br|p]​dr)2/p+2​C2/p​(1εn​∫tt+εn𝔼​[|σr|p]​dr)2/p.\displaystyle\leq 2{\varepsilon}_{n}\left(\frac{1}{{\varepsilon}_{n}}\int_{t}^{t+{\varepsilon}_{n}}\mathbb{E}\left[\left|b_{r}\right|^{p}\right]\,\mathrm{d}r\right)^{2/p}+2C^{2/p}\left(\frac{1}{{\varepsilon}_{n}}\int_{t}^{t+{\varepsilon}_{n}}\mathbb{E}\left[\left|{\sigma}_{r}\right|^{p}\right]\,\mathrm{d}r\right)^{2/p}.(3.33)

Here, we used the Young inequality in the first line and the Burkholder-Davis-Gundy inequality in the second (see, e.g.,[Revuz+Yor_1999_Continuous_martingales_Brownian_motion, Chapter IV, Theorem 4.1]).

The claim follows by arbitrariness ofεn→0+{\varepsilon}_{n}\to 0^{+}.□\Box

## 3.5BSDE-induced convex risk measures

Motivated by the previous subsection, where the restriction of the dynamic entropic risk measure𝔢γ\mathfrak{e}^{\gamma}toLexp​(ℱT)L^{\mathrm{exp}}(\mathcal{F}_{T})admits a representation through the quadratic driverg​(z)=γ2​|z|2g(z)=\frac{\gamma}{2}|z|^{2}, we now turn to the general BSDE framework.
We study resilience evaluation through an outer normalized, cash-additive, and convex dynamic risk measure induced by a family of BSDEs.

## 3.5.1Convex drivers and dual representation

Throughout this section, we fix a driverggthat satisfies any of the following two conditions.
- (𝐂𝐋)\mathbf{(C_{L})}

Letg:Ω×[0,T]×ℝm→ℝg:{\Omega}\times[0,T]\times\mathbb{R}^{m}\to\mathbb{R}be a driver such that
- ∙\bullet

ggisℙ⊗ℓ1\mathbb{P}\otimes\ell_{1}-a.e. convex inℝm\mathbb{R}^{m}.
- ∙\bullet

g​(⋅,0)=0g(\,\cdot\,,0)=0,ℙ⊗ℓ1\mathbb{P}\otimes\ell_{1}-a.e.
- ∙\bullet

There existsL≥0L\geq 0such thatℙ⊗ℓ1\mathbb{P}\otimes\ell_{1}-a.e.,|g​(⋅,z)−g​(⋅,z′)|≤L​|z−z′|,∀z,z′∈ℝm.\displaystyle|g(\,\cdot\,,z)-g(\,\cdot\,,z^{\prime})|\leq L|z-z^{\prime}|,\qquad\forall\,z,z^{\prime}\in\mathbb{R}^{m}.(3.34)
- (𝐂𝐐)\mathbf{(C_{Q})}

Letg:Ω×[0,T]×ℝm→ℝg:{\Omega}\times[0,T]\times\mathbb{R}^{m}\to\mathbb{R}be a driver such that
- ∙\bullet

ggisℙ⊗ℓ1\mathbb{P}\otimes\ell_{1}-a.e. convex inℝm\mathbb{R}^{m}.
- ∙\bullet

g​(⋅,0)=0g(\,\cdot\,,0)=0,ℙ⊗ℓ1\mathbb{P}\otimes\ell_{1}-a.e.
- ∙\bullet

There existk>0k>0such thatℙ⊗ℓ1\mathbb{P}\otimes\ell_{1}-a.e.,|g​(⋅,z)|≤k​(1+|z|2),∀z∈ℝm.\displaystyle|g(\,\cdot\,,z)|\leq k(1+|z|^{2}),\qquad\forall\,z\in\mathbb{R}^{m}.(3.35)

We first present a technical lemma that provides the predictable selectorsg∗,Π,Θg^{\ast},\Pi,\Thetafor the convex conjugate ofgg, for the orthogonal projector onto∂zg​(⋅,0)\partial_{z}g(\,\cdot\,,0), and for the subgradient∂zg​(⋅,z)\partial_{z}g(\,\cdot\,,z), respectively.
The proof can be found in AppendixB.

## Lemma 11.

There exist𝒫⊗ℬ​(ℝm)\mathcal{P}\otimes\mathscr{B}(\mathbb{R}^{m})-measurable mapsg∗:Ω×[0,T]×ℝm→ℝ∪{+∞},Π,Θ:Ω×[0,T]×ℝm→ℝmg^{\ast}:{\Omega}\times[0,T]\times\mathbb{R}^{m}\to\mathbb{R}\cup\{+\infty\},\qquad\qquad\Pi,\Theta:{\Omega}\times[0,T]\times\mathbb{R}^{m}\to\mathbb{R}^{m}

such that the following properties holdℙ⊗ℓ1\mathbb{P}\otimes\ell_{1}-a.e.
- (i)

The mapq↦g∗​(⋅,q)q\mapsto g^{\ast}(\,\cdot\,,q)is the convex conjugate ofz↦g​(⋅,z)z\mapsto g(\,\cdot\,,z), namelyg∗​(⋅,q)=supz∈ℝm{q⋅z−g​(⋅,z)},∀q∈ℝm.g^{\ast}(\,\cdot\,,q)=\sup_{z\in\mathbb{R}^{m}}\big\{q\cdot z-g(\,\cdot\,,z)\big\},\qquad\forall\,q\in\mathbb{R}^{m}.(3.36)

Moreover:
- (a)

It is proper, convex, lower semi-continuous and non-negative.
In addition,
- ∙\bullet

ifggsatisfies(𝐂𝐐)\mathbf{(C_{Q})}, theng∗​(⋅,q)≥|q|24​k−k\displaystyle g^{\ast}(\,\cdot\,,q)\geq\frac{|q|^{2}}{4k}-kfor allq∈ℝmq\in\mathbb{R}^{m}.
- ∙\bullet

ifggsatisfies(𝐂𝐋)\mathbf{(C_{L})}, theng∗​(⋅,q)=+∞\displaystyle g^{\ast}(\,\cdot\,,q)=+\inftyfor all|q|>L|q|>L.
- (b)

It holds:g∗∗​(⋅,z):=supq∈ℝm{q⋅z−g∗​(⋅,q)}=g​(⋅,z),∀z∈ℝm.g^{\ast\ast}(\,\cdot\,,z):=\sup_{q\in\mathbb{R}^{m}}\big\{q\cdot z-g^{\ast}(\,\cdot\,,q)\big\}=g(\,\cdot\,,z),\qquad\forall\,z\in\mathbb{R}^{m}.
- (c)

∂zg​(⋅,0)\partial_{z}g(\,\cdot\,,0)is a non-empty, closed, convex,ℙ⊗ℓ1\mathbb{P}\otimes\ell_{1}-uniformly bounded set, and it coincides with the zero-set ofq↦g∗​(⋅,q)q\mapsto g^{\ast}(\,\cdot\,,q), i.e.,q∈∂zg​(⋅,0)⟺g∗​(⋅,q)=0.q\in\partial_{z}g(\,\cdot\,,0)\qquad\Longleftrightarrow\qquad g^{\ast}(\,\cdot\,,q)=0.

In particular, for anyq∈∂zg​(⋅,0)q\in\partial_{z}g(\,\cdot\,,0), we have|q|≤L|q|\leq Lifggsatisfies(𝐂𝐋)\mathbf{(C_{L})}and|q|≤2​k|q|\leq 2kifggsatisfies(𝐂𝐐)\mathbf{(C_{Q})}.
- (ii)

The mapq↦Π​(⋅,q)q\mapsto\Pi(\,\cdot\,,q)is the orthogonal projector onto∂zg​(⋅,0)\partial_{z}g(\,\cdot\,,0), namely:Π​(⋅,q)∈∂zg​(⋅,0),|q−Π​(⋅,q)|=dist⁡(q,∂zg​(⋅,0)).\Pi(\,\cdot\,,q)\in\partial_{z}g(\,\cdot\,,0),\qquad\big|q-\Pi(\,\cdot\,,q)\big|=\operatorname{dist}\big(q,\partial_{z}g(\,\cdot\,,0)\big).(3.37)
- (iii)

For anyz∈ℝmz\in\mathbb{R}^{m}, we haveΘ​(⋅,z)∈∂zg​(⋅,z)\Theta(\,\cdot\,,z)\in\partial_{z}g(\,\cdot\,,z), i.e.,g​(⋅,z)=Θ​(⋅,z)⋅z−g∗​(⋅,Θ​(⋅,z))g(\,\cdot\,,z)=\Theta(\,\cdot\,,z)\cdot z-g^{\ast}\big(\,\cdot\,,\Theta(\,\cdot\,,z)\big).
Moreover,|Θ​(⋅,z)|≤L|\Theta(\,\cdot\,,z)|\leq Lifggsatisfies(𝐂𝐋)\mathbf{(C_{L})}, and|Θ​(⋅,z)|≤5​k​(1+|z|)|\Theta(\,\cdot\,,z)|\leq 5k(1+|z|)ifggsatisfies(𝐂𝐐)\mathbf{(C_{Q})}.

The following lemma gathers some integrability estimates under the probability measureℚμ\mathbb{Q}^{\mu}defined in Section2.1.
The proof is classical, hence postponed to AppendixB.

## Lemma 12.

Forμ∈BMOT\mu\in\mathrm{BMO}_{T}and0≤a≤b≤T0\leq a\leq b\leq T, defineℰa,bμ:=ℰbμℰaμ=exp⁡(∫abμr⋅dWr−12​∫ab|μr|2​dr).\mathcal{E}_{a,b}^{\mu}:=\frac{\mathcal{E}_{b}^{\mu}}{\mathcal{E}_{a}^{\mu}}=\exp\bigg(\int_{a}^{b}\mu_{r}\cdot\,\mathrm{d}W_{r}-\frac{1}{2}\int_{a}^{b}|\mu_{r}|^{2}\,\mathrm{d}r\bigg).
- (i)

For every fixedμ∈BMOT\mu\in\mathrm{BMO}_{T}, andp∈[1,+∞)p\in[1,+\infty)Lexp⊂Lexp​(ℚμ)⊂Lp​(ℚμ).L^{\mathrm{exp}}\subset L^{\mathrm{exp}}(\mathbb{Q}^{\mu})\subset L^{p}(\mathbb{Q}^{\mu}).
- (ii)

LetK>0K>0and let𝒰⊆BMOT\mathcal{U}\subseteq\mathrm{BMO}_{T}be such that|μ|≤K|\mu|\leq K,ℙ⊗ℓ1​-a.e.\mathbb{P}\otimes\ell_{1}\text{-a.e.}, for allμ∈𝒰\mu\in\mathcal{U}.
Then, for everyp>1p>1, and0≤a≤b≤T0\leq a\leq b\leq T,𝔼​[(ℰa,bμ)p|ℱa]≤exp⁡(p2​(p−1)​K2​T),∀μ∈𝒰.\mathbb{E}\big[(\mathcal{E}_{a,b}^{\mu})^{p}\big|\mathcal{F}_{a}\big]\leq\exp\Big(\frac{p}{2}(p-1)K^{2}T\Big),\qquad\forall\,\mu\in\mathcal{U}.
- (iii)

Under the assumptions of(ii),
for every1≤u<p1\leq u<p, and everys∈[0,T]s\in[0,T],(𝔼μ​[|X|u|ℱs])1/u≤exp⁡(K2​T2​(p−u))​(𝔼​[|X|p|ℱs])1/p,∀μ∈𝒰,X∈Lp.\big(\mathbb{E}_{\mu}[|X|^{u}|\mathcal{F}_{s}]\big)^{1/u}\leq\exp\bigg(\frac{K^{2}T}{2(p-u)}\bigg)\big(\mathbb{E}[|X|^{p}|\mathcal{F}_{s}]\big)^{1/p},\qquad\forall\,\mu\in\mathcal{U},\ X\in L^{p}.

In particular,LpL^{p}is continuously embedded inLu​(ℚμ)L^{u}(\mathbb{Q}^{\mu}),
uniformly inμ∈𝒰\mu\in\mathcal{U}.

We now turn to the properties of the dynamic risk measureρ{\rho}induced by the drivergg, and on its dual representation in particular.
The assertions of the following proposition are mainly standard.
The only point that needs a separate argument is the extension of the dual representation to unbounded terminal conditions with fixed dual classBMOT\mathrm{BMO}_{T}.
Related dual representations for unbounded terminal conditions are available in broader frameworks; see[Drapeau+Kupper+RosazzaGianin+Tangpi_2016_Dual_representation_minimal_supersolutions_convex_BSDEs, Theorems 4.5, 4.6]and[11, Theorem 3.1(i)].
These results, however, are formulated over broader dual classes, which may depend on the terminal condition, and therefore do not directly yield the representation over the fixed classBMOT\mathrm{BMO}_{T}established below.
The proof is in AppendixB.

## Proposition 13.

Assume thatggsatisfies either(𝐂𝐋)\mathbf{(C_{L})}or(𝐂𝐐)\mathbf{(C_{Q})}.
Then the risk measureρ:𝒳T→𝒳t{\rho}:\mathcal{X}_{T}\to\mathcal{X}_{t}induced byggis normalized, cash-additive, convex and enjoys the following dual representation for alls∈[0,T]s\in[0,T]:ρs​(X)=ess​supμ∈ℬ⁡𝔼μ​[X−∫sTg∗​(r,μr)​dr|ℱs],∀X∈𝒳T,{\rho}_{s}(X)=\operatorname*{ess\,sup}_{\mu\in\mathcal{B}}\mathbb{E}_{\mu}\left[\left.X-\int_{s}^{T}g^{\ast}(r,\mu_{r})\,\mathrm{d}r\right|\mathcal{F}_{s}\right],\qquad\forall\,X\in\mathcal{X}_{T},(3.38)

where:
- •

Ifggsatisfies(𝐂𝐋)\mathbf{(C_{L})}, we have𝒳T=L2​(ℱT)\mathcal{X}_{T}=L^{2}(\mathcal{F}_{T})andℬ\mathcal{B}is the set of all predictable processesμ:Ω×[0,T]→ℝm\mu:{\Omega}\times[0,T]\to\mathbb{R}^{m}such that|μ|≤L|\mu|\leq L,ℙ⊗ℓ1\mathbb{P}\otimes\ell_{1}-a.e.
- •

Ifggsatisfies(𝐂𝐐)\mathbf{(C_{Q})}, we have𝒳T=Lexp​(ℱT)\mathcal{X}_{T}=L^{\mathrm{exp}}(\mathcal{F}_{T})andℬ:=BMOT\mathcal{B}:=\mathrm{BMO}_{T}.

The conditional expectation in (3.38)

## Remark 17.

The assumption(Hs,t)drives the localization of the dual representation (3.38) onto the zero-penalty set∂zg​(⋅,0)\partial_{z}g(\,\cdot\,,0)defining𝒜0\mathcal{A}^{0}.
More precisely, the factor1/ε1/{\varepsilon}makes the contribution of controls with a non-negligible conditional penalty on either(s,t)(s,t)or(t,t+ε)(t,t+{\varepsilon})asymptotically negligible, as shown inStep 3of the proof.
On(s,t)(s,t), condition (3.39) then converts the smallness of the penalty into proximity to∂zg​(⋅,0)\partial_{z}g(\,\cdot\,,0).
This leads to the zero-penalty class𝒜0\mathcal{A}^{0}in the statement.
The additional requirement that the auxiliary controls in𝒜s\mathcal{A}_{s}vanish on[0,s][0,s]is unrelated to this localization and is used only to simplify the change-of-measure estimates and the pasting argument in the proof.

## 3.5.3Proof of the main theorem

Before proceeding to the proof of Theorem15, we need a few preliminary lemmas.
The following lemma quantifies the sensitivity of conditional expectations underℚμ\mathbb{Q}^{\mu}to perturbations of the dual controlμ\mu.
It provides the key stability estimate for the localization argument.
Its technical proof is deferred to AppendixBto preserve the flow of the exposition.

## Lemma 21.

Let𝒰⊆BMOT\mathcal{U}\subseteq\mathrm{BMO}_{T}be non-empty.

and that𝒰\mathcal{U}is stable underℱs\mathcal{F}_{s}-pasting, namely,μ1​𝟙A+μ2​𝟙Ac∈𝒰,∀μ1,μ2∈𝒰,A∈ℱs.\mu^{1}\mathds{1}_{A}+\mu^{2}\mathds{1}_{A^{c}}\in\mathcal{U},\qquad\forall\,\mu^{1},\mu^{2}\in\mathcal{U},\quad A\in\mathcal{F}_{s}.(3.48)

Then for every non-negativeX∈L2X\in L^{2},∥ess​supμ∈𝒰𝔼μ[X|ℱs]∥L1≤eK2​T/2∥X∥L2.\left\|\operatorname*{ess\,sup}_{\mu\in\mathcal{U}}\mathbb{E}_{\mu}[X|\mathcal{F}_{s}]\right\|_{L^{1}}\leq e^{K^{2}T/2}\|X\|_{L^{2}}.

## Proof.

By Lemma12(iii), applied withu=1u=1andp=2p=2,∥𝔼μ[X|ℱs]∥L1≤eK2​T/2∥X∥L2,∀μ∈𝒰.\left\|\mathbb{E}_{\mu}[X|\mathcal{F}_{s}]\right\|_{L^{1}}\leq e^{K^{2}T/2}\|X\|_{L^{2}},\qquad\forall\,\mu\in\mathcal{U}.

Let us denote by𝒬\mathcal{Q}the set of all probabilitiesℚμ\mathbb{Q}^{\mu}onℱ\mathcal{F}asμ\muvaries in𝒰\mathcal{U}.
Sinceμ=0\mu=0onΩ×[0,s]{\Omega}\times[0,s], we haveℚμ=ℙ\mathbb{Q}^{\mu}=\mathbb{P}onℱs\mathcal{F}_{s}for allμ∈𝒰\mu\in\mathcal{U}.
Moreover, we show that the set𝒬\mathcal{Q}satisfies the following pasting property:(d​ℚ1d​ℙ​𝟙A+d​ℚ2d​ℙ​𝟙Ac)​ℙ∈𝒬,∀ℚ1,ℚ2∈𝒬,A∈ℱs.\displaystyle\left(\frac{\,\mathrm{d}\mathbb{Q}^{1}}{\,\mathrm{d}\mathbb{P}}\mathds{1}_{A}+\frac{\,\mathrm{d}\mathbb{Q}^{2}}{\,\mathrm{d}\mathbb{P}}\mathds{1}_{A^{c}}\right)\mathbb{P}\in\mathcal{Q},\qquad\forall\,\mathbb{Q}^{1},\mathbb{Q}^{2}\in\mathcal{Q},\ A\in\mathcal{\mathcal{F}}_{s}.(3.49)

Indeed, fori=1,2i=1,2, ifℚi∈𝒬\mathbb{Q}^{i}\in\mathcal{Q}, there existsμi∈𝒰\mu^{i}\in\mathcal{U}such thatℚi=ℚμi\mathbb{Q}^{i}=\mathbb{Q}^{\mu^{i}}.
FixA∈ℱsA\in\mathcal{F}_{s}.
The property (3.48) in the hypothesis yieldsμ:=μ1​𝟙A+μ2​𝟙Ac∈𝒰\mu:=\mu^{1}\mathds{1}_{A}+\mu^{2}\mathds{1}_{A^{c}}\in\mathcal{U}.
Henceℚμ∈𝒬\mathbb{Q}^{\mu}\in\mathcal{Q}, where the density ofℚμ\mathbb{Q}^{\mu}is exactlyd​ℚμd​ℙ=d​ℚ1d​ℙ​𝟙A+d​ℚ2d​ℙ​𝟙Ac,\frac{\,\mathrm{d}\mathbb{Q}^{\mu}}{\,\mathrm{d}\mathbb{P}}=\frac{\,\mathrm{d}\mathbb{Q}^{1}}{\,\mathrm{d}\mathbb{P}}\mathds{1}_{A}+\frac{\,\mathrm{d}\mathbb{Q}^{2}}{\,\mathrm{d}\mathbb{P}}\mathds{1}_{A^{c}},

which shows the pasting property (3.49) for the set𝒬\mathcal{Q}.

These properties allow us to apply[21, Lemma 3.4]and infer the existence of a sequence(μn)n∈ℕ∈𝒰ℕ(\mu^{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}\in\mathcal{U}^{\mathbb{N}}such thatYn:=𝔼μn​[X|ℱs]⟶ess​supμ∈𝒰⁡𝔼μ​[X|ℱs]:=Y,ℙ​-a.s.,Y_{n}:=\mathbb{E}_{\mu^{n}}[X|\mathcal{F}_{s}]\longrightarrow\operatorname*{ess\,sup}_{\mu\in\mathcal{U}}\mathbb{E}_{\mu}[X|\mathcal{F}_{s}]:=Y,\qquad\mathbb{P}\text{-a.s.},

andYn≤Yn+1Y_{n}\leq Y_{n+1}for alln∈ℕn\in\mathbb{N}.
SinceX≥0X\geq 0, the monotone convergence theorem yields∥Y∥L1=limn→∞∥𝔼μn[X|ℱs]∥L1≤eK2​T/2∥X∥L2.\|Y\|_{L^{1}}=\lim_{n\to\infty}\left\|\mathbb{E}_{\mu^{n}}[X|\mathcal{F}_{s}]\right\|_{L^{1}}\leq e^{K^{2}T/2}\|X\|_{L^{2}}.

□\Box

We are now ready for the proof of the main Theorem15.
We will give the details only under its assumption(b), postponing to AppendixDthe case(a).
Although the representation in Theorem15is stated over the global zero-penalty class𝒜0\mathcal{A}^{0}, throughout the proof we work with the normalized class𝒜s\mathcal{A}_{s}defined in (3.45). This normalization is needed for the change-of-measure estimates and the pasting argument.
Remark16shows that the two classes yield the same essential supremum for theℱt\mathcal{F}_{t}-measurable random variables considered below.

In order to provide an easy reference to the reader, let us introduce the notations that will be used throughout the proof.

Let us now outline the proof, which is organized in seven steps.

InStep11, we rewrite the increments of the processπ\pivia Girsanov’s theorem, then use the dual representation (3.38) for the risk measureρ{\rho}, obtaining1ε​ρs​(πt+ε−πt)=ess​supμ∈ℬ⁡Vεs,t​(μ).\frac{1}{{\varepsilon}}{\rho}_{s}(\pi_{t+{\varepsilon}}-\pi_{t})=\operatorname*{ess\,sup}_{\mu\in\mathcal{B}}V_{\varepsilon}^{s,t}(\mu).

Ifμ∈ℬεs,t\mu\in\mathcal{B}_{\varepsilon}^{s,t}, theng∗​(⋅,μ)=0g^{\ast}(\,\cdot\,,\mu)=0onΩ×[t+ε,T]{\Omega}\times[t+{\varepsilon},T].
Therefore, this step allows us to restrict the time integration in the penalty term in (3.38) from[s,T][s,T]to[s,t+ε][s,t+{\varepsilon}].

InStep33, we perform the main asymptotic localization.
We split the penalty term into its contribution on[s,t][s,t]and on[t,t+ε][t,t+{\varepsilon}], and we show that the conditional payoff functionVεs,t​(μ)V_{\varepsilon}^{s,t}(\mu)diverges to−∞-\infty, asε→0+{\varepsilon}\to 0^{+}, as soon as any of these two contributions is larger thanε\sqrt{\varepsilon}.

This means that, asymptotically, the leading contribution to the essential supremum comes from processesμ∈𝒞εs,t\mu\in\mathcal{C}_{\varepsilon}^{s,t}.

InStep 4, we derive an upper bound for the essential supremum ofVεs,t​(μ)V_{\varepsilon}^{s,t}(\mu), forμ∈𝒞εs,t\mu\in\mathcal{C}_{\varepsilon}^{s,t}.

## 3.5.4Attainment of the essential supremum

This section is devoted to the attainment of the essential supremum in formula (3.41).
We first need a technical lemma to construct the support function of the zero-penalty set∂zg​(⋅,0)\partial_{z}g(\,\cdot\,,0)together with a measurable maximizer.
Since the proof relies on standard techniques in convex analysis, we postponed it to AppendixB.

Fix an arbitraryμ∈𝒜0\mu\in\mathcal{A}^{0}, and defineWrμ:=Wr−∫0rμ​dℓ1W^{\mu}_{r}:=W_{r}-\int_{0}^{r}\mu\,\mathrm{d}\ell_{1}forr∈[0,T]r\in[0,T].
ThenWμW^{\mu}is a Brownian motion underℚμ\mathbb{Q}^{\mu}.
Evaluating (3.114) atr=sr=sand rewriting the stochastic integral in terms ofWμW^{\mu}, we obtainVs=Yt+∫st(h​(u,Ξu)−μu⋅Ξu)​du−∫stΞu⋅dWuμ.V_{s}=Y_{t}+\int_{s}^{t}\big(h(u,\Xi_{u})-\mu_{u}\cdot\Xi_{u}\big)\,\,\mathrm{d}u-\int_{s}^{t}\Xi_{u}\cdot\,\mathrm{d}W^{\mu}_{u}.

Taking theℚμ\mathbb{Q}^{\mu}-conditional expectation with respect toℱs\mathcal{F}_{s}givesVs=𝔼μ​[Yt+∫st(h​(u,Ξu)−μu⋅Ξu)​du|ℱs].V_{s}=\mathbb{E}_{\mu}\!\left[Y_{t}+\int_{s}^{t}\big(h(u,\Xi_{u})-\mu_{u}\cdot\Xi_{u}\big)\,\,\mathrm{d}u\,\bigg|\,\mathcal{F}_{s}\right].(3.116)

Sinceμ∈∂zg​(⋅,0)\mu\in\partial_{z}g(\,\cdot\,,0),ℙ⊗ℓ1\mathbb{P}\otimes\ell_{1}-a.e. onΩ×(s,t){\Omega}\times(s,t),
Lemma22(i)impliesh​(⋅,Ξ)−μ⋅Ξ≥0ℙ⊗ℓ1​-a.e. on​Ω×(s,t).h(\,\cdot\,,\Xi)-\mu\cdot\Xi\geq 0\qquad\mathbb{P}\otimes\ell_{1}\text{-a.e. on }{\Omega}\times(s,t).

HenceVs≥𝔼μ​[Yt|ℱs]V_{s}\geq\mathbb{E}_{\mu}[Y_{t}|\mathcal{F}_{s}].
Taking the essential supremum overμ∈𝒜0\mu\in\mathcal{A}^{0}and using
 (3.41), we obtainVs≥ess​supμ∈𝒜0⁡𝔼μ​[Yt|ℱs]=𝒟sρ​πt.V_{s}\geq\operatorname*{ess\,sup}_{\mu\in\mathcal{A}^{0}}\mathbb{E}_{\mu}[Y_{t}|\mathcal{F}_{s}]=\mathcal{D}^{\rho}_{s}\pi_{t}.(3.117)

Forμ=μ∗\mu=\mu^{\ast}, Lemma22(ii)and the definition (3.115) giveh​(⋅,Ξ)−μ∗⋅Ξ=0ℙ⊗ℓ1​-a.e. on​Ω×(s,t).h(\,\cdot\,,\Xi)-\mu^{\ast}\cdot\Xi=0\qquad\mathbb{P}\otimes\ell_{1}\text{-a.e. on }{\Omega}\times(s,t).

Therefore,Vs=𝔼μ∗​[Yt|ℱs]V_{s}=\mathbb{E}_{\mu^{\ast}}[Y_{t}|\mathcal{F}_{s}].
Sinceμ∗∈𝒜0\mu^{\ast}\in\mathcal{A}^{0}, it follows thatVs=𝔼μ∗​[Yt|ℱs]≤ess​supμ∈𝒜0⁡𝔼μ​[Yt|ℱs]=𝒟sρ​πt≤Vs.V_{s}=\mathbb{E}_{\mu^{\ast}}[Y_{t}|\mathcal{F}_{s}]\leq\operatorname*{ess\,sup}_{\mu\in\mathcal{A}^{0}}\mathbb{E}_{\mu}[Y_{t}|\mathcal{F}_{s}]=\mathcal{D}^{\rho}_{s}\pi_{t}\leq V_{s}.

This proves (3.113).□\Box

## 4Examples of Resilience Evaluations

In this section we consider several examples for the abstract formula obtained in Theorem15, where the outer dynamic risk measureρ{\rho}is induced by a driverggand the inner processπ\pifollows Itô dynamics.
We first discuss the case in whichρ\rhois kept general, whileπ\piis generated by a BSDE.
We then specialize the drivergg, which leads to several relevant examples for the outer coherent and convex risk measureρ\rho.
Finally, we explain the importance of having a BSDE-induced risk measure by showing that Value at Risk and Expected Shortfall fail to generate well-defined resilience evaluations.

## 4.1BSDE-induced inner processes

We consider a general driverggsatisfying(𝐂𝐋)\mathbf{(C_{L})}and the associated dynamic risk measureρ\rhoas in the setting of Theorem15.
We now specify the dynamics for the inner processπ\pi, assuming that it is generated by a BSDE.
LetX∈L2​(ℱT)X\in L^{2}(\mathcal{F}_{T})and let(π,Zπ)(\pi,Z^{\pi})be the solution of the BSDEπu=X+∫uTgπ​(r,πr,Zrπ)​dr−∫uTZrπ⋅dWr,u∈[0,T],\pi_{u}=X+\int_{u}^{T}g^{\pi}(r,\pi_{r},Z^{\pi}_{r})\,\mathrm{d}r-\int_{u}^{T}Z^{\pi}_{r}\cdot\,\mathrm{d}W_{r},\qquad u\in[0,T],

for some drivergπg^{\pi}satisfying(𝐋)\mathbf{(L)}forp=2p=2.
Thenb∈LT2b\in L^{2}_{T}withb=−gπ​(⋅,π,Zπ),σ=Zπ.b=-g^{\pi}(\,\cdot\,,\pi,Z^{\pi}),\qquad{\sigma}=Z^{\pi}.

Then Theorem15states that forℓ1\ell_{1}-a.e.t∈[0,T)t\in[0,T)and alls∈[0,t]s\in[0,t], we have𝒟sρ​πt=ess​maxμ∈𝒜0⁡𝔼μ​[−gπ​(t,πt,Ztπ)+g​(t,Ztπ)|ℱs],\mathcal{D}^{\rho}_{s}\pi_{t}=\operatorname*{ess\,max}_{\mu\in\mathcal{A}^{0}}\mathbb{E}_{\mu}\big[-g^{\pi}(t,\pi_{t},Z^{\pi}_{t})+g(t,Z^{\pi}_{t})\big|\mathcal{F}_{s}\big],

where the equality holds inL2L^{2}and𝒜0\mathcal{A}^{0}was defined in (3.42).
In particular, fors=ts=t,𝒟tρ​πt=−gπ​(t,πt,Ztπ)+g​(t,Ztπ),in​L2​(ℱt),ℓ1​-a.e.​t∈[0,T).\mathcal{D}^{\rho}_{t}\pi_{t}=-g^{\pi}(t,\pi_{t},Z^{\pi}_{t})+g(t,Z^{\pi}_{t}),\qquad\text{ in }L^{2}(\mathcal{F}_{t}),\ \ell_{1}\text{-a.e. }t\in[0,T).

This formula shows explicitly the interaction between the two BSDE drivers.
The drivergπg^{\pi}determines the local drift of the inner risk processπ\pi, whereas the outer driverggcontrols the exposureZtπZ^{\pi}_{t}to the Brownian motion through the external risk measureρ\rho.

The same formulas remain valid when the outer driverggsatisfies the quadratic assumption(𝐂𝐐)\mathbf{(C_{Q})}, provided that the BSDE-induced processπ\pihas bounded Itô coefficients. This boundedness is not a consequence of the standard quadratic BSDE estimates alone, since these usually yieldZπ∈BMOTZ^{\pi}\in\mathrm{BMO}_{T}rather thanZπ∈LT∞Z^{\pi}\in L^{\infty}_{T}.
Nonetheless, sufficient conditions are given by the Markovian Brownian FBSDE setting of[16, Appendix B].

## Example 24(Black–Scholes replicating portfolio).

Assume for simplicity thatm=1m=1and consider a Black–Scholes market with numéraire assetBt=exp⁡(∫0tru​du)B_{t}=\exp\big(\int_{0}^{t}r_{u}\,\mathrm{d}u\big)and risky assetSt=S0+∫0tαu​Su​du+∫0tςu​Su​dWu,t≥0,S_{t}=S_{0}+\int_{0}^{t}\alpha_{u}S_{u}\,\mathrm{d}u+\int_{0}^{t}\varsigma_{u}S_{u}\,\mathrm{d}W_{u},\qquad t\geq 0,

whereS0>0S_{0}>0andr,α,ςr,\alpha,\varsigmaare bounded predictable processes, withς\varsigmabounded away from zero.
Setϑ:=(r−α)/ς\vartheta:=(r-\alpha)/\varsigma.
Thenϑ\varthetais bounded andWϑ:=W−∫0⋅ϑu​duW^{\vartheta}:=W-\int_{0}^{\cdot}\vartheta_{u}\,\mathrm{d}uis aℚϑ\mathbb{Q}^{\vartheta}-Brownian motion by the Girsanov theorem.
LetX∈L2​(ℱT)X\in L^{2}(\mathcal{F}_{T})be the payoff of a European option with exercise timeT>0T>0.
Since the market is complete, the replicating price process isπt=𝔼ϑ​[Bt​BT−1​X|ℱt],t∈[0,T].\pi_{t}=\mathbb{E}_{\vartheta}\big[B_{t}B_{T}^{-1}X\big|\mathcal{F}_{t}\big],\qquad t\in[0,T].

By the martingale representation theorem, there exists a predictable processZπZ^{\pi}such that(π,Zπ)(\pi,Z^{\pi})is the solution of the BSDE with parameters(gπ,T,X)(g^{\pi},T,X), where the driver isgπ​(ω,u,y,z)=−ru​(ω)​y+ϑu​(ω)​z,(ω,u,y,z)∈Ω×[0,T]×ℝ×ℝ,g^{\pi}({\omega},u,y,z)=-r_{u}({\omega})y+\vartheta_{u}({\omega})z,\qquad({\omega},u,y,z)\in{\Omega}\times[0,T]\times\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{R},

and satisfies(𝐋)\mathbf{(L)}forp=2p=2.
The unique self-financing replicating strategy is given byΔ:=Zπ/(ς​S)\Delta:=Z^{\pi}/(\varsigma S),η:=(π−Δ​S)/B\eta:=(\pi-\Delta S)/B, so thatπ=η​B+Δ​S\pi=\eta B+\Delta S.

Therefore, if the outer dynamic risk measureρ\rhois induced by a driverggsatisfying(𝐂𝐋)\mathbf{(C_{L})}, Theorem15yields, forℓ1\ell_{1}-a.e.t∈[0,T)t\in[0,T)and alls∈[0,t]s\in[0,t],𝒟sρ​πt\displaystyle\mathcal{D}^{\rho}_{s}\pi_{t}=ess​maxμ∈𝒜0⁡𝔼μ​[rt​πt−ϑt​Ztπ+g​(t,Ztπ)|ℱs]\displaystyle=\operatorname*{ess\,max}_{\mu\in\mathcal{A}^{0}}\mathbb{E}_{\mu}\big[r_{t}\pi_{t}-\vartheta_{t}Z^{\pi}_{t}+g(t,Z^{\pi}_{t})\big|\mathcal{F}_{s}\big](4.1)=ess​maxμ∈𝒜0⁡𝔼μ​[rt​ηt​Bt+αt​Δt​St+g​(t,ςt​Δt​St)|ℱs].\displaystyle=\operatorname*{ess\,max}_{\mu\in\mathcal{A}^{0}}\mathbb{E}_{\mu}\big[r_{t}\eta_{t}B_{t}+{\alpha}_{t}\Delta_{t}S_{t}+g(t,\varsigma_{t}\Delta_{t}S_{t})\big|\mathcal{F}_{s}\big].

It is clear from the last formula that the resilience evaluation well captures the risk-free raterrfor the non-risky fractionη​B\eta Bof the portfolio, the local rate of evolutionα{\alpha}for the risky portionΔ​S\Delta Sof the portfolio, and an additional term that combines the volatilityς\varsigmaof the risky portion, with the investor’s preferences encoded in the drivergg.

For computational purposes, assumer,α,ςr,{\alpha},\varsigmaconstant in time and deterministic, so thatBt=er​tB_{t}=e^{rt}andSt=S0​exp⁡[(α−ς2/2)​t+ς​Wt]S_{t}=S_{0}\exp[({\alpha}-\varsigma^{2}/2)t+\varsigma W_{t}].

Thenπt=K​e−r​(T−t)​𝒩​(dt−)−St​𝒩​(dt+),Δt=−𝒩​(dt+),ηt=K​e−r​T​𝒩​(dt−),\pi_{t}=Ke^{-r(T-t)}\mathcal{N}(d^{-}_{t})-S_{t}\mathcal{N}(d^{+}_{t}),\qquad\Delta_{t}=-\mathcal{N}(d^{+}_{t}),\qquad\eta_{t}=Ke^{-rT}\mathcal{N}(d^{-}_{t}),

## 4.2Coherent outer risk measures

We here provide some examples of sublinear driversggsatisfying the assumptions of Theorem15.
First, we notice that a sublinear driver is convex and positively homogeneous, thus it induces a coherent risk measureρ\rho.
In addition, a uniformly Lipschitz sublinear driver satisfies the assumption(𝐂𝐋)\mathbf{(C_{L})}, thus, in the following, we fixb∈LT2b\in L^{2}_{T}so that the increments ofπ\piare inL2​(ℱT)L^{2}(\mathcal{F}_{T}).

## Example 25(Linear driver).

The easiest case is obtained by a linear driver,g​(ω,r,z)=βr​(ω)⋅z,(ω,r,z)∈Ω×[0,T]×ℝm,g({\omega},r,z)=\beta_{r}({\omega})\cdot z,\qquad({\omega},r,z)\in{\Omega}\times[0,T]\times\mathbb{R}^{m},

whereβ:Ω×[0,T]→ℝm\beta:{\Omega}\times[0,T]\to\mathbb{R}^{m}is a predictable andℙ⊗ℓ1\mathbb{P}\otimes\ell_{1}-essentially bounded process, and the induced risk measure is a straightforward generalization of Example6, i.e., the conditionalℚβ\mathbb{Q}^{\beta}-expectationρr​(X)=𝔼β​[X|ℱr],X∈L2​(ℱT),r∈[0,T].\rho_{r}(X)=\mathbb{E}_{\beta}[X|\mathcal{F}_{r}],\qquad X\in L^{2}(\mathcal{F}_{T}),\ r\in[0,T].

Forℙ⊗ℓ1\mathbb{P}\otimes\ell_{1}-a.e.(ω,r)∈Ω×[0,T]({\omega},r)\in{\Omega}\times[0,T],g∗​(ω,r,βr​(ω))=0g^{\ast}({\omega},r,\beta_{r}({\omega}))=0, whileg∗​(ω,r,q)=+∞g^{\ast}({\omega},r,q)=+\inftyfor allq≠βr​(ω)q\neq\beta_{r}({\omega}).
Therefore,∂zg​(ω,r,0)={βr​(ω)}\partial_{z}g({\omega},r,0)=\{\beta_{r}({\omega})\}and the condition(Hs,t)is trivially satisfied for any0≤s≤t<T0\leq s\leq t<T, withψ​(x)=x\psi(x)=xforx∈[0,+∞]x\in[0,+\infty].
Then𝒜0={β}\mathcal{A}^{0}=\{\beta\}, hence Theorem15states that, forℓ1\ell_{1}-a.e.t∈[0,T)t\in[0,T)and alls∈[0,t]s\in[0,t],𝒟sρ​πt=𝔼β​[bt+βt⋅σt|ℱs].\mathcal{D}^{\rho}_{s}\pi_{t}=\mathbb{E}_{\beta}[b_{t}+\beta_{t}\cdot{\sigma}_{t}|\mathcal{F}_{s}].

It is worth mentioning that, in this very special situation, the delicate construction of Theorem15is not needed.
Indeed, the same result can be proved by using Girsanov’s theorem to rewrite the Itô dynamics forπ\piin terms of aℚβ\mathbb{Q}^{\beta}-Brownian motion, which then simplifies in the computation of theℚβ\mathbb{Q}^{\beta}-conditional expectation that definesρ{\rho}.
Lemma1then gives the convergence asε→0+{\varepsilon}\to 0^{+}.

## Example 26(Sublinear driver onℝ\mathbb{R}).

Another trivial example is the general sublinear driverg:ℝ→ℝg:\mathbb{R}\to\mathbb{R}, which has the analytic expressiong​(z)=a​z++b​z−={a​zif​z≥0,−b​zif​z<0,g(z)=az^{+}+bz^{-}=\begin{cases}az\qquad&\text{ if }z\geq 0,\\
-bz&\text{ if }z<0,\end{cases}

for somea,b≥0a,b\geq 0, and generates the coherent risk measureρr​(X)=ess​maxμ∈𝒜[−b,a]⁡𝔼μ​[X|ℱr],r∈[0,T],X∈L2​(ℱT),\displaystyle\rho_{r}(X)=\operatorname*{ess\,max}_{\mu\in\mathcal{A}^{[-b,a]}}\mathbb{E}_{\mu}[X|\mathcal{F}_{r}],\qquad r\in[0,T],\ X\in L^{2}(\mathcal{F}_{T}),(4.5)𝒜[−b,a]:={μ:Ω×[0,T]→ℝ​predictable,−b≤μ≤a},\displaystyle\mathcal{A}^{[-b,a]}:=\big\{\mu:{\Omega}\times[0,T]\to\mathbb{R}\text{ predictable},\ -b\leq\mu\leq a\big\},(4.6)

In this case∂g​(0)=[−b,a]\partial g(0)=[-b,a], hence𝒜0=𝒜[−b,a]\mathcal{A}^{0}=\mathcal{A}^{[-b,a]}.
Set for everyt∈[0,T)t\in[0,T),𝒜t[−b,a]:={μ∈𝒜[−b,a]:−b≤μt≤aℙ​-a.s.}.\mathcal{A}_{t}^{[-b,a]}:=\big\{\mu\in\mathcal{A}^{[-b,a]}\ :\ -b\leq\mu_{t}\leq a\quad\mathbb{P}\text{-a.s.}\big\}.

The condition(Hs,t)is satisfied thanks to Remark14(v)(v), for any0≤s≤t<T0\leq s\leq t<T.
Therefore, forℓ1\ell_{1}-a.e.t∈[0,T)t\in[0,T)and alls∈[0,t]s\in[0,t]𝒟sρ​πt=ess​maxμ∈𝒜[−b,a]⁡𝔼μ​[bt+a​σt++b​σt−|ℱs]=ess​maxμ∈𝒜t[−b,a]⁡𝔼μ​[bt+σt​μt|ℱs],\mathcal{D}^{\rho}_{s}\pi_{t}=\operatorname*{ess\,max}_{\mu\in\mathcal{A}^{[-b,a]}}\mathbb{E}_{\mu}[b_{t}+a{\sigma}_{t}^{+}+b{\sigma}_{t}^{-}|\mathcal{F}_{s}]=\operatorname*{ess\,max}_{\mu\in\mathcal{A}_{t}^{[-b,a]}}\mathbb{E}_{\mu}\left[b_{t}+{\sigma}_{t}\mu_{t}\big|\mathcal{F}_{s}\right],
