## Example 27(General sublinear uniformly Lipschitz driver).

Consider the most general sublinear driver satisfying assumption(𝐂𝐋)\mathbf{(C_{L})}g​(ω,r,z)=supq∈C​(ω,r)q⋅z,(ω,r,z)∈Ω×[0,T]×ℝm,g({\omega},r,z)=\sup_{q\in C({\omega},r)}q\cdot z,\qquad({\omega},r,z)\in{\Omega}\times[0,T]\times\mathbb{R}^{m},

whereC​(ω,r)C({\omega},r)is a closed, non-empty, convex, and uniformly bounded subset ofℝm\mathbb{R}^{m}such that{(ω,r,q):q∈C​(ω,r)}\{({\omega},r,q)\ :\ q\in C({\omega},r)\}is𝒫⊗ℬ​(ℝm)\mathcal{P}\otimes\mathscr{B}(\mathbb{R}^{m})-measurable.
Then∂zg​(ω,r,0)=C​(ω,r)\partial_{z}g({\omega},r,0)=C({\omega},r)by the representation of sublinear functions as support functions (see, e.g.,[Rockafellar_1997_Convex_analysis, Corollary 13.1.1]).
In this case the convex conjugate is0onC​(ω,r)C({\omega},r)and+∞+\inftyoutside it, and therefore the associated dynamic risk measure is coherent and admits the dual representationρr​(X)=ess​maxμ∈𝒜C⁡𝔼μ​[X|ℱr],r∈[0,T],X∈L2​(ℱT),\displaystyle\rho_{r}(X)=\operatorname*{ess\,max}_{\mu\in\mathcal{A}^{C}}\mathbb{E}_{\mu}[X|\mathcal{F}_{r}],\qquad r\in[0,T],\ X\in L^{2}(\mathcal{F}_{T}),(4.7)𝒜C:={μ:Ω×[0,T]→ℝm​predictable,μu​(ω)∈C​(ω,u),ℙ⊗ℓ1​-a.e.​(ω,u)},\displaystyle\mathcal{A}^{C}:=\Bigl\{\mu:{\Omega}\times[0,T]\to\mathbb{R}^{m}\text{ predictable},\ \mu_{u}({\omega})\in C({\omega},u),\mathbb{P}\otimes\ell_{1}\text{-a.e. }({\omega},u)\Bigr\},(4.8)

Moreover, for0≤s≤t<T0\leq s\leq t<T, the condition(Hs,t)is satisfied with, for instance,ψ​(x)=x\psi(x)=xforx∈[0,+∞]x\in[0,+\infty], and𝒜0=𝒜C\mathcal{A}^{0}=\mathcal{A}^{C}.
For everyt∈[0,T)t\in[0,T), also set𝒜tC:={μ∈𝒜C:μt​(ω)∈C​(ω,t)ℙ​-a.e.​ω}\mathcal{A}_{t}^{C}:=\big\{\mu\in\mathcal{A}^{C}:\ \mu_{t}({\omega})\in C({\omega},t)\quad\mathbb{P}\text{-a.e. }{\omega}\big\}.
Then Theorem15yields𝒟sρ​πt=ess​maxμ∈𝒜C⁡𝔼μ​[bt+supq∈C​(t)q⋅σt|ℱs]=ess​maxμ∈𝒜tC⁡𝔼μ​[bt+σt⋅μt|ℱs],\mathcal{D}^{\rho}_{s}\pi_{t}=\operatorname*{ess\,max}_{\mu\in\mathcal{A}^{C}}\mathbb{E}_{\mu}\bigg[b_{t}+\sup_{q\in C(t)}q\cdot\sigma_{t}\,\bigg|\,\mathcal{F}_{s}\bigg]=\operatorname*{ess\,max}_{\mu\in\mathcal{A}_{t}^{C}}\mathbb{E}_{\mu}\left[b_{t}+\sigma_{t}\cdot\mu_{t}\big|\mathcal{F}_{s}\right],

forℓ1\ell_{1}-a.e.t∈[0,T)t\in[0,T)and everys∈[0,t]s\in[0,t], where the last equality follows from
Corollary19.

## 4.3Convex outer risk measures

We now give some examples of genuinely convex, non-coherent risk measures, induced by drivers satisfying(𝐂𝐐)\mathbf{(C_{Q})}that are not positively homogeneous.
In what follows, we work with a fixedπ\piwithb,|σ|∈LT∞b,|{\sigma}|\in L^{\infty}_{T}.

## Example 28(Entropy).

Consider the entropic driverg​(z)=γ2​|z|2,g(z)=\frac{\gamma}{2}|z|^{2},for someγ>0\gamma>0and allz∈ℝmz\in\mathbb{R}^{m}.
Its convex conjugate isg∗​(q)=12​γ​|q|2g^{\ast}(q)=\frac{1}{2\gamma}|q|^{2}for allq∈ℝmq\in\mathbb{R}^{m}and the associated risk measureρ=𝔢γ\rho=\mathfrak{e}^{\gamma}is the usual dynamic entropy with risk-aversion coefficientγ>0{\gamma}>0, see Section3.4.
In this case∂zg​(0)={0}\partial_{z}g(0)=\{0\}and the condition(Hs,t)is satisfied by Remark14(v)(v), for all0≤s≤t<T0\leq s\leq t<T.
The admissible set appearing in Theorem15reduces to𝒜0={0}\mathcal{A}^{0}=\{0\}and consequently𝒟s𝔢γ​πt=𝔼​[bt+γ2​|σt|2|ℱs],ℓ1​-a.e.​t∈[0,T),∀s∈[0,t].\mathcal{D}^{\mathfrak{e}^{\gamma}}_{s}\pi_{t}=\mathbb{E}\Big[b_{t}+\frac{{\gamma}}{2}|{\sigma}_{t}|^{2}\Big|\mathcal{F}_{s}\Big],\qquad\ell_{1}\text{-a.e. }t\in[0,T),\ \forall\,s\in[0,t].(4.9)

Thus, under the stronger boundedness assumptions imposed in this subsection, Theorem15recovers the formula established in Theorem9.

Suppose in addition that the inner processπ\piis the dynamic entropic risk evaluation with risk aversion coefficientδ>0\delta>0, possibly different fromγ\gamma, of a certain positionX∈Lexp​(ℱT)X\in L^{\exp}(\mathcal{F}_{T}).
In this case, we writeπ=𝔢δ​(X)\pi=\mathfrak{e}^{\delta}(X), we denote the solution of the BSDE inducing𝔢δ\mathfrak{e}^{\delta}by(𝔢δ​(X),Zδ)(\mathfrak{e}^{\delta}(X),Z^{\delta}), and the coefficientsb,σb,{\sigma}are given byσ=Zδ\sigma=Z^{\delta}andb=−δ​|Zδ|2/2b=-\delta|Z^{\delta}|^{2}/2, see also Section3.4and Example24.
We then rewrite the formula in (4.9) as follows:𝒟s𝔢γ​(𝔢tδ​(X))=γ−δ2​𝔼​[|Ztδ|2|ℱs]=−γ−δδ​𝒟s𝔼​(𝔢tδ​(X)),\mathcal{D}^{\mathfrak{e}^{\gamma}}_{s}\big(\mathfrak{e}^{\delta}_{t}(X)\big)=\frac{\gamma-\delta}{2}\mathbb{E}\big[|Z^{\delta}_{t}|^{2}\big|\mathcal{F}_{s}\big]=-\frac{\gamma-\delta}{\delta}\mathcal{D}^{\mathbb{E}}_{s}\big(\mathfrak{e}^{\delta}_{t}(X)\big),

where in the last equality we used the formula for the resilience evaluation through𝔼[⋅|ℱs]\mathbb{E}[\,\cdot\,|\mathcal{F}_{s}], see Example6.
It shows that the resilience evaluation of𝔢tδ​(X)\mathfrak{e}_{t}^{\delta}(X)through the entropic risk measure is a rescaled version of the resilience rate of𝔢tδ​(X)\mathfrak{e}_{t}^{\delta}(X).
In particular, whenδ=γ\delta=\gamma, then the resilience evaluation vanishes.

## 4.4Counterexamples

We now provide counterexamples showing that the existence of the resilience evaluation may fail outside the BSDE-induced, normalized, and cash-additive framework.
We consider separately two types of failures: first, risk measures such as Value at Risk and Expected Shortfall, that do not admit a BSDE representation; second, BSDE-induced risk measures that are not normalized or cash-additive.
In each case, we show that the resilience evaluation may be ill-posed even for very simple Itô processesπ\pi.

First, let us set the conventions by recalling the definitions.

## Definition 31.

Assume thatp>1p>1,X∈LpX\in L^{p}, andα∈(0,1)\alpha\in(0,1).
The Value at Risk of the positionXXat levelα\alphais defined as:VaRα⁡(X):=inf{x∈ℝ:ℙ​(X≤x)>α}.\operatorname{VaR}_{\alpha}(X):=\inf\left\{x\in\mathbb{R}\ :\ \mathbb{P}(X\leq x)>{\alpha}\right\}.

The Expected Shortfall ofXXof levelα\alphais defined as:ESα⁡(X):=11−α​∫α1VaRγ⁡(X)​dγ.\operatorname{ES}_{\alpha}(X):=\frac{1}{1-{\alpha}}\int_{\alpha}^{1}\operatorname{VaR}_{\gamma}(X)\,\mathrm{d}\gamma.

## Example 32(VaR and ES).

We show that, forα∈(0,1/2)∪(1/2,1){\alpha}\in(0,1/2)\cup(1/2,1), there existsπ\pisuch that forℓ1\ell_{1}-a.e.t∈[0,T)t\in[0,T), asε→0+{\varepsilon}\to 0^{+}:1ε​VaRα⁡(πt+ε−πt)⟶±∞,1ε​ESα⁡(πt+ε−πt)⟶+∞,\frac{1}{{\varepsilon}}\operatorname{VaR}_{\alpha}(\pi_{t+{\varepsilon}}-\pi_{t})\longrightarrow\pm\infty,\qquad\frac{1}{{\varepsilon}}\operatorname{ES}_{\alpha}(\pi_{t+{\varepsilon}}-\pi_{t})\longrightarrow+\infty,

where the±\pmsign depends on the levelα\alpha.
Indeed, ifπ=W\pi=W, then we have:1ε​VaRα⁡(πt+ε−πt)=1ε​VaRα⁡(Wt+ε−Wt)=1ε​VaRα⁡(Wε)=ε−1/2​Φ−1​(α),\displaystyle\frac{1}{{\varepsilon}}\operatorname{VaR}_{\alpha}(\pi_{t+{\varepsilon}}-\pi_{t})=\frac{1}{{\varepsilon}}\operatorname{VaR}_{\alpha}(W_{t+{\varepsilon}}-W_{t})=\frac{1}{{\varepsilon}}\operatorname{VaR}_{\alpha}(W_{{\varepsilon}})={\varepsilon}^{-1/2}\Phi^{-1}{(\alpha)},

whereΦ\Phiis the normal CDF and the last expression diverges to+∞+\infty(resp.−∞-\infty) forα∈(1/2,1){\alpha}\in(1/2,1)(resp.α∈(0,1/2){\alpha}\in(0,1/2)).
We have a similar behavior for the expected shortfall, which instead yields:1ε​ESα⁡(πt+ε−πt)=1ε​ESα⁡(Wε)=ε−1/2​φ​(Φ−1​(α))1−α⟶+∞,as​ε→0+,\displaystyle\frac{1}{{\varepsilon}}\operatorname{ES}_{\alpha}(\pi_{t+{\varepsilon}}-\pi_{t})=\frac{1}{{\varepsilon}}\operatorname{ES}_{\alpha}(W_{{\varepsilon}})={\varepsilon}^{-1/2}\frac{\varphi\left(\Phi^{-1}{(\alpha)}\right)}{1-\alpha}\longrightarrow+\infty,\text{ as }{\varepsilon}\to 0^{+},

for anyα∈(0,1){\alpha}\in(0,1), whereφ\varphiis the normal PDF.

## Example 33(Necessity of normalization).

Let us consider a BSDE-induced dynamic risk measureρ\rhoassociated with a driverggsatisfying all the assumptions in(𝐂𝐋)\mathbf{(C_{L})}, but withℙ⊗ℓ1​(g​(⋅,0)≠0)>0\mathbb{P}\otimes\ell_{1}\big(g(\,\cdot\,,0)\neq 0\big)>0.
Thenρ\rhois not normalized: there existss∈[0,T)s\in[0,T)withρs​(0)≠0\rho_{s}(0)\neq 0.
As a consequence, the resilience evaluation through thisρ\rhoof even the simplest, constant Itô processπ≡0\pi\equiv 0is ill-posed.
Indeed, for allt∈[s,T)t\in[s,T), we have‖1ε​ρs​(πt+ε−πt)‖L1=1ε​‖ρs​(0)‖L1⟶∞.\left\|\frac{1}{{\varepsilon}}\rho_{s}(\pi_{t+{\varepsilon}}-\pi_{t})\right\|_{L^{1}}=\frac{1}{{\varepsilon}}\|\rho_{s}(0)\|_{L^{1}}\longrightarrow\infty.

The obstruction is exactly the lack of normalization: the null increment is assigned a non-zero risk, which is then amplified by the scaling factorε−1{\varepsilon}^{-1}.

## Example 34(Necessity of cash-additivity).

Leta>0a>0and consider the Lipschitz driverg​(y)=a​|y|g(y)=a|y|fory∈ℝy\in\mathbb{R}.
The induced dynamic risk measureρs​(X)=ess​supμ∈𝒜a⁡𝔼​[e∫sTμr​dr​X|ℱs],X∈L2​(ℱT),s∈[0,T],\rho_{s}(X)=\operatorname*{ess\,sup}_{\mu\in\mathcal{A}^{a}}\mathbb{E}\Big[e^{\int_{s}^{T}\mu_{r}\,\mathrm{d}r}X\Big|\mathcal{F}_{s}\Big],\qquad X\in L^{2}(\mathcal{F}_{T}),\ s\in[0,T],

where𝒜a:={μ:Ω×[0,T]→[−a,a]​predictable}\mathcal{A}^{a}:=\big\{\mu:{\Omega}\times[0,T]\to[-a,a]\text{ predictable}\big\}, is not cash-additive.
Take the simple Itô processπ=W\pi=WonΩ×[0,T]{\Omega}\times[0,T], and fix0≤s≤t<T0\leq s\leq t<T.
Forε∈(0,T−t]{\varepsilon}\in(0,T-t], the incrementπt+ε−πt=Wt+ε−Wt=:ξε\pi_{t+{\varepsilon}}-\pi_{t}=W_{t+{\varepsilon}}-W_{t}=:\xi_{\varepsilon}is normally distributed and independent ofℱs\mathcal{F}_{s}.
Choosing the admissible controlμ¯:=a​sign⁡(ξε)​𝟙(t+ε,T]∈𝒜a,\bar{\mu}:=a\operatorname{sign}(\xi_{\varepsilon})\mathds{1}_{(t+{\varepsilon},T]}\in\mathcal{A}^{a},gives1ε​ρs​(πt+ε−πt)≥1ε​𝔼​[ea​(T−t−ε)​ξε+−e−a​(T−t−ε)​ξε−|ℱs]=12​π​ε​(ea​(T−t−ε)−e−a​(T−t−ε)),\frac{1}{{\varepsilon}}\rho_{s}(\pi_{t+{\varepsilon}}-\pi_{t})\geq\frac{1}{{\varepsilon}}\mathbb{E}\left[e^{a(T-t-{\varepsilon})}\xi_{\varepsilon}^{+}-e^{-a(T-t-{\varepsilon})}\xi_{\varepsilon}^{-}\bigg|\mathcal{F}_{s}\right]=\frac{1}{\sqrt{2\pi{\varepsilon}}}\left(e^{a(T-t-{\varepsilon})}-e^{-a(T-t-{\varepsilon})}\right),

where the right-hand side diverges to+∞+\inftyasε→0+{\varepsilon}\to 0^{+}.

## 5Conclusion

This paper introduced a local notion of financial resilience based on the infinitesimal behavior of dynamic risk measures.
We showed that the resilience evaluation for general Itô processes via BSDE-induced, cash-additive, normalized, and convex risk measures is well-defined and admits an explicit representation.
The examples and counterexamples clarify both the scope of the construction and the role of the structural assumptions imposed on the risk measure.

Several natural extensions remain open.
A first direction is to pass from the Brownian filtration considered here to a Brownian–Poisson filtration, allowing both the underlying processes and the risk evaluations to exhibit jumps.
A second direction is to replace the deterministic-time formulation by a formulation indexed by stopping times, which would make the resilience evaluation more flexible for applications involving random horizons and event-driven risk assessment.

because‖Rn‖L1≤𝔼​[|R​(Xn)|]\|R_{n}\|_{L^{1}}\leq\mathbb{E}[|R(X_{n})|].

## Proof of Proposition10.

LetA∈𝒫A\in\mathcal{P}withℙ⊗ℓ1​(A)=0\mathbb{P}\otimes\ell_{1}(A)=0be such that the properties listed in(𝐂𝐋)\mathbf{(C_{L})}or(𝐂𝐐)\mathbf{(C_{Q})}hold inAc:=(Ω×[0,T])∖AA^{c}:=({\Omega}\times[0,T])\setminus A.

Then(i)holds by construction.

For every(ω,r)∈Ac({\omega},r)\in A^{c},
the mapz↦g​(ω,r,z)z\mapsto g({\omega},r,z)is continuous onℝm\mathbb{R}^{m}because it is finite and convex onℝm\mathbb{R}^{m}.
Hence, for every(ω,r,q)∈Ω×[0,T]×ℝm({\omega},r,q)\in{\Omega}\times[0,T]\times\mathbb{R}^{m},g∗​(ω,r,q):=𝟙Ac​(ω,r)​supz∈ℝm{q⋅z−g​(ω,r,z)}=𝟙Ac​(ω,r)​supz∈ℚm{q⋅z−g​(ω,r,z)}.g^{\ast}({\omega},r,q):=\mathds{1}_{A^{c}}({\omega},r)\sup_{z\in\mathbb{R}^{m}}\big\{q\cdot z-g({\omega},r,z)\big\}=\mathds{1}_{A^{c}}({\omega},r)\sup_{z\in\mathbb{Q}^{m}}\big\{q\cdot z-g({\omega},r,z)\big\}.

SinceA∈𝒫A\in\mathcal{P}, and(ω,r,q)↦q⋅z−g​(ω,r,z)({\omega},r,q)\mapsto q\cdot z-g({\omega},r,z)is𝒫⊗ℬ​(ℝm)\mathcal{P}\otimes\mathscr{B}(\mathbb{R}^{m})-measurable for every fixedz∈ℚmz\in\mathbb{Q}^{m}, it follows thatg∗g^{\ast}is𝒫⊗ℬ​(ℝm)\mathcal{P}\otimes\mathscr{B}(\mathbb{R}^{m})-measurable.

Fix(ω,r)∈Ac({\omega},r)\in A^{c}.
The mapq↦g∗​(ω,r,q)q\mapsto g^{\ast}({\omega},r,q)is proper, convex and lower semi-continuous as it is the convex conjugate of the finite and convex functionz↦g​(ω,r,z)z\mapsto g({\omega},r,z)(see, e.g.,[Rockafellar_1997_Convex_analysis, Theorem 12.2.]).
Moreover,(i)(b)holds by continuity ofz↦g​(ω,r,z)z\mapsto g({\omega},r,z).
Sinceg​(ω,r,0)=0g({\omega},r,0)=0, we also haveg∗​(ω,r,q)≥q⋅0−g​(ω,r,0)=0,∀q∈ℝm,g^{\ast}({\omega},r,q)\geq q\cdot 0-g({\omega},r,0)=0,\qquad\forall\,q\in\mathbb{R}^{m},

sog∗g^{\ast}is non-negative.
This proves the first part of(i)(a).
Concerning the first item in(i)(a), ifggsatisfies(𝐂𝐐)\mathbf{(C_{Q})}, thenq⋅z−g​(ω,r,z)≥q⋅z−k​(1+|z|2)q\cdot z-g({\omega},r,z)\geq q\cdot z-k(1+|z|^{2})for allq,z∈ℝmq,z\in\mathbb{R}^{m}.

Instead, ifggsatisfies(𝐂𝐋)\mathbf{(C_{L})}, thenq⋅z−g​(ω,r,z)≥q⋅z−L​|z|q\cdot z-g({\omega},r,z)\geq q\cdot z-L|z|for allq,z∈ℝmq,z\in\mathbb{R}^{m}.
Fix|q|>L|q|>Land setzn:=n​q/|q|z_{n}:=n\,q/|q|forn∈ℕn\in\mathbb{N}.
Theng∗​(ω,r,q)≥supn∈ℕ{q⋅zn−L​|zn|}=supn∈ℕn​(|q|−L)=+∞.g^{\ast}({\omega},r,q)\geq\sup_{n\in\mathbb{N}}\big\{q\cdot z_{n}-L|z_{n}|\big\}=\sup_{n\in\mathbb{N}}n(|q|-L)=+\infty.

This completes the proof of(i)(a).

The subdifferential∂zg​(ω,r,0)\partial_{z}g({\omega},r,0)is a non-empty, closed and convex set becausez↦g​(ω,r,z)z\mapsto g({\omega},r,z)is convex and finite onℝm\mathbb{R}^{m}(see, e.g.,[Rockafellar_1997_Convex_analysis, Theorem 23.4]).

Sinceg​(ω,r,0)=0g({\omega},r,0)=0, this is equivalent toq⋅z−g​(ω,r,z)≤0q\cdot z-g({\omega},r,z)\leq 0for allz∈ℝmz\in\mathbb{R}^{m}, namely tog∗​(ω,r,q)≤0g^{\ast}({\omega},r,q)\leq 0.

It remains to prove the uniform boundedness of∂zg​(ω,r,0)\partial_{z}g({\omega},r,0).
If∂zg​(ω,r,0)={0}\partial_{z}g({\omega},r,0)=\{0\}, then this is trivial.
Thus fixq∈∂zg​(ω,r,0)q\in\partial_{z}g({\omega},r,0)such thatq≠0q\neq 0.
Ifggsatisfies(𝐂𝐋)\mathbf{(C_{L})}then, recalling (B.1):q⋅z≤g​(ω,r,z)≤|g​(ω,r,z)|=|g​(ω,r,z)−g​(ω,r,0)|≤L​|z|,∀z∈ℝm.q\cdot z\leq g({\omega},r,z)\leq|g({\omega},r,z)|=|g({\omega},r,z)-g({\omega},r,0)|\leq L|z|,\qquad\forall\,z\in\mathbb{R}^{m}.

If insteadggsatisfies(𝐂𝐐)\mathbf{(C_{Q})}then:q⋅z≤g​(ω,r,z)≤|g​(ω,r,z)|≤k​(1+|z|2),∀z∈ℝm.q\cdot z\leq g({\omega},r,z)\leq|g({\omega},r,z)|\leq k(1+|z|^{2}),\qquad\forall\,z\in\mathbb{R}^{m}.

Then the previous inequalities yieldλ​|q|≤L​λ,∀λ>0⟹|q|≤L\lambda|q|\leq L{\lambda},\qquad\forall\,\lambda>0\qquad\Longrightarrow\qquad|q|\leq L

in the Lipschitz case, andλ​|q|≤k​(1+λ2),∀λ>0⟹|q|≤infλ>0k​(1λ+λ)=2​k\lambda|q|\leq k(1+\lambda^{2}),\qquad\forall\,\lambda>0\qquad\Longrightarrow\qquad|q|\leq\inf_{\lambda>0}k\Big(\frac{1}{\lambda}+\lambda\Big)=2k

in the quadratic case.
This proves(i)(c).

Finally we prove(ii).
Thanks to(i)(a)and(i)(c), the set∂zg​(ω,r,0)\partial_{z}g(\omega,r,0)is convex, closed and non-empty for all(ω,r)∈Ac({\omega},r)\in A^{c}.
Hence, the projectorℝm∋q↦Π​(ω,r,q)∈∂zg​(ω,r,0)\mathbb{R}^{m}\ni q\mapsto\Pi({\omega},r,q)\in\partial_{z}g({\omega},r,0)is well-defined, is characterized by (3.37) and is continuous for all(ω,r)∈Ac({\omega},r)\in A^{c}.
DefineΠ​(ω,r,q)=0∈ℝm\Pi({\omega},r,q)=0\in\mathbb{R}^{m}for(ω,r,q)∈A×ℝm({\omega},r,q)\in A\times\mathbb{R}^{m}.
Then the mapΩ×[0,T]∋(ω,r)↦Π​(ω,r,q)∈ℝm{\Omega}\times[0,T]\ni({\omega},r)\mapsto\Pi({\omega},r,q)\in\mathbb{R}^{m}is predictable for anyq∈ℝmq\in\mathbb{R}^{m}by[Rockafellar+Wets_1998_Variational_analysis, Theorem 14.37, Example 14.29].

It remains to prove(iii).
SetE:=Ω×[0,T]×ℝmE:=\Omega\times[0,T]\times\mathbb{R}^{m}andℰ:=𝒫⊗ℬ​(ℝm)\mathscr{E}:=\mathcal{P}\otimes\mathscr{B}(\mathbb{R}^{m}).
Consider the functionf:E×ℝm∋((ω,r,z),q)↦{g​(ω,r,q),if​(ω,r)∈Ac,0,if​(ω,r)∈A.f:E\times\mathbb{R}^{m}\ni(({\omega},r,z),q)\mapsto\begin{cases}g({\omega},r,q),&\text{if }({\omega},r)\in A^{c},\\
0,&\text{if }(\omega,r)\in A.\end{cases}

\{0\},&\text{if }(\omega,r)\in A.\end{cases}

By the measurable selection theorem[Rockafellar+Wets_1998_Variational_analysis, Corollary 14.6], there exists a𝒫⊗ℬ​(ℝm)\mathcal{P}\otimes\mathscr{B}(\mathbb{R}^{m})-measurable selectorΘ:Ω×[0,T]×ℝm→ℝm\Theta:\Omega\times[0,T]\times\mathbb{R}^{m}\to\mathbb{R}^{m}such thatΘ​(ω,r,z)∈Γ​(ω,r,z)\Theta(\omega,r,z)\in\Gamma(\omega,r,z)for all(ω,r,z)∈Ω×[0,T]×ℝm(\omega,r,z)\in\Omega\times[0,T]\times\mathbb{R}^{m}.
Hence, for(ω,r)∈Ac({\omega},r)\in A^{c},Θ​(ω,r,z)∈∂zg​(ω,r,z),∀z∈ℝm,\Theta(\omega,r,z)\in\partial_{z}g(\omega,r,z),\qquad\forall\,z\in\mathbb{R}^{m},

which is equivalent tog​(⋅,z)+g∗​(⋅,Θ​(⋅,z))=Θ​(⋅,z)⋅z,∀z∈ℝm,g(\,\cdot\,,z)+g^{\ast}\big(\,\cdot\,,\Theta(\,\cdot\,,z)\big)=\Theta(\,\cdot\,,z)\cdot z,\qquad\forall\,z\in\mathbb{R}^{m},

by[Rockafellar_1997_Convex_analysis, Theorem 23.5].

It remains to prove the stated bounds onΘ\Theta.
Fix(ω,r)∈Ac(\omega,r)\in A^{c},z∈ℝmz\in\mathbb{R}^{m}andq∈∂zg​(ω,r,z)q\in\partial_{z}g(\omega,r,z).

and therefore|q|≤L|q|\leq L.

If insteadggsatisfies(𝐂𝐐)\mathbf{(C_{Q})}, thenλ​|q|≤g​(ω,r,z+λ​u)−g​(ω,r,z)≤k​(1+|z+λ​u|2)+k​(1+|z|2)≤k​(2+2​|z|2+2​λ​|z|+λ2),\lambda|q|\leq g(\omega,r,z+\lambda u)-g(\omega,r,z)\leq k(1+|z+\lambda u|^{2})+k(1+|z|^{2})\leq k\big(2+2|z|^{2}+2\lambda|z|+\lambda^{2}\big),

where we used the triangle inequality in the end.

where we used1+|z|2≤1+|z|\sqrt{1+|z|^{2}}\leq 1+|z|and2≤2+2​2≤52\leq 2+2\sqrt{2}\leq 5.
By arbitrariness ofq∈∂zg​(ω,r,z)q\in\partial_{z}g(\omega,r,z), and sinceΘ​(ω,r,z)∈∂zg​(ω,r,z)\Theta(\omega,r,z)\in\partial_{z}g(\omega,r,z), this proves the claimed bound and completes the proof of(iii).□\Box

## Proof of(i).

Fixμ∈BMOT\mu\in\mathrm{BMO}_{T}.
Then there existsr>1r>1such thatℰTμ∈Lr\mathcal{E}_{T}^{\mu}\in L^{r}, thanks to[Kazamaki_1994_Continuous_exponential_martingales_BMO, Theorem 3.4].
Letr′:=r/(r−1)r^{\prime}:=r/(r-1).
IfX∈LexpX\in L^{\mathrm{exp}}, then, for everyc>0c>0,𝔼μ​[ec​|X|]=𝔼​[ℰTμ​ec​|X|]≤‖ℰTμ‖Lr​(𝔼​[ec​r′​|X|])1/r′<+∞.\mathbb{E}_{\mu}\big[e^{c|X|}\big]=\mathbb{E}\Big[\mathcal{E}_{T}^{\mu}\,e^{c|X|}\Big]\leq\big\|\mathcal{E}_{T}^{\mu}\big\|_{L^{r}}\left(\mathbb{E}\Big[e^{cr^{\prime}|X|}\Big]\right)^{1/r^{\prime}}<+\infty.

ThereforeX∈Lexp​(ℚμ)X\in L^{\mathrm{exp}}(\mathbb{Q}^{\mu}). Since finite exponential moments of every order imply finite polynomial moments of every order, we also haveX∈Lp​(ℚμ)X\in L^{p}(\mathbb{Q}^{\mu})for everyp∈[1,+∞)p\in[1,+\infty).

## Proof of(ii).

Recall thatℰ​(M):=exp⁡(M−12​⟨M⟩)\mathcal{E}(M):=\exp\left(M-\frac{1}{2}\langle M\rangle\right)denotes the stochastic exponential of any continuous martingaleMMwithM0=0M_{0}=0, where⟨M⟩\langle M\rangleis its quadratic variation process (see, e.g.,[Kazamaki_1994_Continuous_exponential_martingales_BMO, Section 1.1]).
Then for allp>0p>0and0≤a≤b≤T0\leq a\leq b\leq T, we have𝔼​[(ℰb​(M))p|ℱa]\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[(\mathcal{E}_{b}(M))^{p}|\mathcal{F}_{a}\right]=𝔼​[exp⁡(p​Mb−p2​⟨M⟩b)|ℱa]\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}\left[\left.\exp{\left(pM_{b}-\frac{p}{2}\langle M\rangle_{b}\right)}\right|\mathcal{F}_{a}\right](B.2)=𝔼​[ℰb​(p​M)​exp⁡((p22−p2)​⟨M⟩b)|ℱa].\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}\left[\left.\mathcal{E}_{b}(pM)\exp{\left(\Big(\frac{p^{2}}{2}-\frac{p}{2}\Big)\langle M\rangle_{b}\right)}\right|\mathcal{F}_{a}\right].(B.3)

If, in addition,⟨M⟩b\langle M\rangle_{b}is bounded, then Novikov’s condition is satisfied andℰ​(p​M)\mathcal{E}(pM)is a martingale.
Therefore we reach𝔼​[(ℰb​(M))p|ℱa]≤ℰa​(p​M)​exp⁡(12​p​(p−1)​‖⟨M⟩b‖L∞).\mathbb{E}\left[(\mathcal{E}_{b}(M))^{p}|\mathcal{F}_{a}\right]\leq\mathcal{E}_{a}(pM)\exp{\left(\frac{1}{2}p(p-1)\|\langle M\rangle_{b}\|_{L^{\infty}}\right)}.

The assertion follows by applying this inequality to the martingaleM:=∫0⋅𝟙[a,T]​μ⋅dWM:=\int_{0}^{\,\cdot}\mathds{1}_{[a,T]}\mu\cdot\,\mathrm{d}W, which satisfiesℰb​(M)=ℰa,bμ\mathcal{E}_{b}(M)=\mathcal{E}_{a,b}^{\mu},ℰa​(p​M)=1\mathcal{E}_{a}(pM)=1, and⟨M⟩b≤K2​(b−a)≤K2​T\langle M\rangle_{b}\leq K^{2}(b-a)\leq K^{2}T.

By the conditional change-of-measure formula, and the conditional Hölder inequality with conjugate exponentsp/u>1p/u>1andq:=p/(p−u)>1q:=p/(p-u)>1, we have𝔼μ​[|X|u|ℱs]=𝔼​[ℰs,Tμ​|X|u|ℱs]≤(𝔼​[(ℰs,Tμ)q|ℱs])1/q​(𝔼​[|X|p|ℱs])u/p.\mathbb{E}_{\mu}[|X|^{u}|\mathcal{F}_{s}]=\mathbb{E}[\mathcal{E}^{\mu}_{s,T}|X|^{u}|\mathcal{F}_{s}]\leq(\mathbb{E}[(\mathcal{E}^{\mu}_{s,T})^{q}|\mathcal{F}_{s}])^{1/q}(\mathbb{E}[|X|^{p}|\mathcal{F}_{s}])^{u/p}.

## Proof of Lemma20..

Fixμ,ν∈BMOT\mu,\nu\in\mathrm{BMO}_{T}.
By the Girsanov theorem,Wμ:=W−∫0⋅μr​drW^{\mu}:=W-\int_{0}^{\,\cdot}\mu_{r}\,\mathrm{d}ris aℚμ\mathbb{Q}^{\mu}-Brownian motion (see, e.g.,[Karatzas+Shreve_1991_Brownian_motion_stochastic_calculus, Section 3.5, Theorem 5.1]).
Let us compute the Radon-Nikodym derivative ofℚν\mathbb{Q}^{\nu}with respect toℚμ\mathbb{Q}^{\mu}, rewrite it in terms ofWμW^{\mu}, and denote it byRTR_{T}:RT\displaystyle R_{T}:=d​ℚνd​ℚμ=d​ℚνd​ℙ​d​ℙd​ℚμ=ℰTν​(ℰTμ)−1\displaystyle:=\frac{\,\mathrm{d}\mathbb{Q}^{\nu}}{\,\mathrm{d}\mathbb{Q}^{\mu}}=\frac{\,\mathrm{d}\mathbb{Q}^{\nu}}{\,\mathrm{d}\mathbb{P}}\frac{\,\mathrm{d}\mathbb{P}}{\,\mathrm{d}\mathbb{Q}^{\mu}}=\mathcal{E}^{\nu}_{T}(\mathcal{E}^{\mu}_{T})^{-1}(B.13)=exp⁡(∫0T(νr−μr)⋅dWr−12​∫0T(|νr|2−|μr|2)​dr)\displaystyle=\exp\left(\int_{0}^{T}(\nu_{r}-\mu_{r})\cdot\,\mathrm{d}W_{r}-\frac{1}{2}\int_{0}^{T}\big(|\nu_{r}|^{2}-|\mu_{r}|^{2}\big)\,\mathrm{d}r\right)(B.14)=exp⁡(∫0T(νr−μr)⋅dWrμ+∫0Tμr⋅(νr−μr)​dr−12​∫0T(|νr|2−|μr|2)​dr)\displaystyle=\exp\left(\int_{0}^{T}(\nu_{r}-\mu_{r})\cdot\,\mathrm{d}W^{\mu}_{r}+\int_{0}^{T}\mu_{r}\cdot(\nu_{r}-\mu_{r})\,\mathrm{d}r-\frac{1}{2}\int_{0}^{T}\big(|\nu_{r}|^{2}-|\mu_{r}|^{2}\big)\,\mathrm{d}r\right)(B.15)=exp⁡(∫0T(νr−μr)⋅dWrμ−12​∫0T|νr−μr|2​dr)=ℰT​((ν−μ)∙Wμ),\displaystyle=\exp\left(\int_{0}^{T}(\nu_{r}-\mu_{r})\cdot\,\mathrm{d}W^{\mu}_{r}-\frac{1}{2}\int_{0}^{T}|\nu_{r}-\mu_{r}|^{2}\,\mathrm{d}r\right)=\mathcal{E}_{T}\big((\nu-\mu)\mathbin{\vbox{\hbox{\tiny$\bullet$}}}W^{\mu}\big),(B.16)

where we used the identity|x−y|2=|x|2+|y|2−2​x⋅y|x-y|^{2}=|x|^{2}+|y|^{2}-2x\cdot y, forx,y∈ℝmx,y\in\mathbb{R}^{m}, to rewrite the Lebesgue integral.
Sinceμ,ν∈BMOT\mu,\nu\in\mathrm{BMO}_{T},M:=(μ∙W)M:=(\mu\mathbin{\vbox{\hbox{\tiny$\bullet$}}}W)andN:=((μ−ν)∙W)N:=\big((\mu-\nu)\mathbin{\vbox{\hbox{\tiny$\bullet$}}}W\big)are BMO-martingales.
By[Kazamaki_1994_Continuous_exponential_martingales_BMO, Theorem 3.6],⟨N,M⟩−N=((ν−μ)∙Wμ)\langle N,M\rangle-N=\big((\nu-\mu)\mathbin{\vbox{\hbox{\tiny$\bullet$}}}W^{\mu}\big)is a BMO-martingale underℚμ=ℰT​(M)​ℙ\mathbb{Q}^{\mu}=\mathcal{E}_{T}(M)\mathbb{P}.
Henceℰ​(⟨N,M⟩−N)=R\mathcal{E}(\langle N,M\rangle-N)=Ris aℚμ\mathbb{Q}^{\mu}-martingale by[Kazamaki_1994_Continuous_exponential_martingales_BMO, Theorem 2.3].
In particular,𝔼μ​[RT|ℱs]=Rs>0\mathbb{E}_{\mu}[R_{T}|\mathcal{F}_{s}]=R_{s}>0for anys∈[0,T]s\in[0,T].

Proof of(i).Assume thatμ,ν∈BMOT\mu,\nu\in\mathrm{BMO}_{T}satisfy|μ|,|ν|≤K|\mu|,|\nu|\leq K,ℙ⊗ℓ1\mathbb{P}\otimes\ell_{1}-a.e. for someK>0K>0, and fixu>1u>1,X∈Lu​(ℚμ)X\in L^{u}(\mathbb{Q}^{\mu}),s∈[0,T]s\in[0,T].

Using the conditional change-of-measure formula𝔼ν​[X|ℱs]=𝔼μ​[RT​Rs−1​X|ℱs]\mathbb{E}_{\nu}[X|\mathcal{F}_{s}]=\mathbb{E}_{\mu}[R_{T}R_{s}^{-1}X|\mathcal{F}_{s}], and the conditional Hölder inequality, we have|𝔼ν[X|ℱs]−𝔼μ[X|ℱs]|u\displaystyle\big|\mathbb{E}_{\nu}[X|\mathcal{F}_{s}]-\mathbb{E}_{\mu}[X|\mathcal{F}_{s}]\big|^{u}=|𝔼μ[(RTRs−1−1)X|ℱs]|u\displaystyle=\left|\mathbb{E}_{\mu}\big[(R_{T}R_{s}^{-1}-1)X\big|\mathcal{F}_{s}\big]\right|^{u}(B.17)≤𝔼μ​[|X|u|ℱs]​(𝔼μ​[|RT​Rs−1−1|v|ℱs])u/v.\displaystyle\leq\mathbb{E}_{\mu}[|X|^{u}|\mathcal{F}_{s}]\Big(\mathbb{E}_{\mu}\big[|R_{T}R_{s}^{-1}-1|^{v}\big|\mathcal{F}_{s}\big]\Big)^{u/v}.(B.18)

Lettingθ:=ν−μ\theta:=\nu-\muandΛ:=ℰ​(𝟙[s,T]​θ∙Wμ)\Lambda:=\mathcal{E}\big(\mathds{1}_{[s,T]}\theta\mathbin{\vbox{\hbox{\tiny$\bullet$}}}W^{\mu}\big), note that we haveRT​Rs−1=ΛTR_{T}R_{s}^{-1}=\Lambda_{T}, hence the Itô formula for the stochastic exponential yieldsRT​Rs−1=1+∫sTΛr​θr⋅dWrμ.R_{T}R_{s}^{-1}=1+\int_{s}^{T}\Lambda_{r}\theta_{r}\cdot\,\mathrm{d}W^{\mu}_{r}.

By the conditional Burkholder-Davis-Gundy inequality (see, e.g.,[Dellacherie+Meyer_1982_Probabilities_potential_B, Chapter VII, numbers 91,92]; see also[Lenglart+Lepingle+Pratelli_1980_Presentation_unifiee_certaines_inegalites_theorie_martingales]), there is a constantcv>0c_{v}>0such that𝔼μ​[|RT​Rs−1−1|v|ℱs]\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{\mu}\big[|R_{T}R_{s}^{-1}-1|^{v}\big|\mathcal{F}_{s}\big]≤cv​𝔼μ​[(∫sTΛr2​|θr|2​dr)v/2|ℱs]\displaystyle\leq c_{v}\mathbb{E}_{\mu}\left[\left.\left(\int_{s}^{T}\Lambda_{r}^{2}|\theta_{r}|^{2}\,\mathrm{d}r\right)^{v/2}\right|\mathcal{F}_{s}\right](B.19)≤cv​𝔼μ​[(supr∈[s,T]Λrv)​(∫sT|θr|2​dr)v/2|ℱs]\displaystyle\leq c_{v}\mathbb{E}_{\mu}\left[\left.\left(\sup_{r\in[s,T]}\Lambda_{r}^{v}\right)\left(\int_{s}^{T}|\theta_{r}|^{2}\,\mathrm{d}r\right)^{v/2}\right|\mathcal{F}_{s}\right](B.20)≤cv​𝔼μ​[(supr∈[s,T]Λr2​v)|ℱs]​𝔼μ​[(∫sT|θr|2​dr)v|ℱs],\displaystyle\leq c_{v}\sqrt{\mathbb{E}_{\mu}\left[\left.\left(\sup_{r\in[s,T]}\Lambda_{r}^{2v}\right)\right|\mathcal{F}_{s}\right]}\sqrt{\mathbb{E}_{\mu}\left[\left.\left(\int_{s}^{T}|\theta_{r}|^{2}\,\mathrm{d}r\right)^{v}\right|\mathcal{F}_{s}\right]},\qquad(B.21)

where we used the conditional Cauchy-Schwarz inequality in the last line.
Since(Λr2​v)r∈[s,T](\Lambda_{r}^{2v})_{r\in[s,T]}is a non-negativeℚμ\mathbb{Q}^{\mu}-sub-martingale, the conditional Doob inequality yields the existence of a universal constantCv>0C_{v}>0such that𝔼μ​[supr∈[s,T]Λr2​v|ℱs]≤Cv​𝔼μ​[ΛT2​v|ℱs]≤Cv​exp⁡(4​v​(2​v−1)​K2​T),\mathbb{E}_{\mu}\left[\left.\sup_{r\in[s,T]}\Lambda_{r}^{2v}\right|\mathcal{F}_{s}\right]\leq C_{v}\mathbb{E}_{\mu}\left[\left.\Lambda_{T}^{2v}\right|\mathcal{F}_{s}\right]\leq C_{v}\exp\left(4v(2v-1)K^{2}T\right),(B.22)

where we used Lemma12(ii)for the second inequality.
Reinserting (B.22) into (B.21) and then again into (B.18) yields the claim with constantCu,K,T:=(cv2​Cv​e4​v​(2​v−1)​K2​T)u/(2​v)>0C_{u,K,T}:=\big(c_{v}^{2}C_{v}e^{4v(2v-1)K^{2}T}\big)^{u/(2v)}>0.

Proof of(ii).Assume now thatμ,ν∈BMOT\mu,\nu\in\mathrm{BMO}_{T}satisfyν=μ=0\nu=\mu=0onΩ×[0,s]{\Omega}\times[0,s]and fixs∈[0,T]s\in[0,T],X∈L∞X\in L^{\infty}.
ThenRs=1R_{s}=1and, by the conditional change-of-measure formula,𝔼ν​[X|ℱs]=𝔼μ​[RT​Rs−1​X|ℱs]=𝔼μ​[RT​X|ℱs]\mathbb{E}_{\nu}[X|\mathcal{F}_{s}]=\mathbb{E}_{\mu}[R_{T}R_{s}^{-1}X|\mathcal{F}_{s}]=\mathbb{E}_{\mu}[R_{T}X|\mathcal{F}_{s}].
Moreover, sinceℚν\mathbb{Q}^{\nu}andℙ\mathbb{P}are equivalent,X∈L∞​(ℚν)X\in L^{\infty}(\mathbb{Q}^{\nu})with‖X‖L∞=‖X‖L∞​(ℚν)\|X\|_{L^{\infty}}=\|X\|_{L^{\infty}(\mathbb{Q}^{\nu})}.
Therefore,|𝔼μ[X|ℱs]−𝔼ν[X|ℱs]|=|𝔼μ[(1−RT)X|ℱs]|≤∥X∥L∞𝔼μ[|RT−1||ℱs].\big|\mathbb{E}_{\mu}[X|\mathcal{F}_{s}]-\mathbb{E}_{\nu}[X|\mathcal{F}_{s}]\big|=\left|\mathbb{E}_{\mu}\big[(1-R_{T})X\big|\mathcal{F}_{s}\big]\right|\leq\|X\|_{L^{\infty}}\mathbb{E}_{\mu}[|R_{T}-1||\mathcal{F}_{s}].(B.23)

Notice thatRT>0R_{T}>0, and estimate the last conditional expectation via the Cauchy-Schwarz inequality𝔼μ​[|RT−1||ℱs]\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{\mu}\big[|R_{T}-1|\big|\mathcal{F}_{s}\big]=𝔼μ​[|RT−1|​|RT+1||ℱs]\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}_{\mu}\Big[\big|\sqrt{R_{T}}-1\big|\,\big|\sqrt{R_{T}}+1\big|\Big|\mathcal{F}_{s}\Big](B.24)≤(𝔼μ​[(RT−1)2|ℱs])1/2​(𝔼μ​[(RT+1)2|ℱs])1/2\displaystyle\leq\left(\mathbb{E}_{\mu}\left[\left.\big(\sqrt{R_{T}}-1\big)^{2}\right|\mathcal{F}_{s}\right]\right)^{1/2}\left(\mathbb{E}_{\mu}\left[\left.\big(\sqrt{R_{T}}+1\big)^{2}\right|\mathcal{F}_{s}\right]\right)^{1/2}(B.25)

Concerning the second factor, we compute the square, then use𝔼μ​[RT|ℱs]=Rs=1\mathbb{E}_{\mu}[R_{T}|\mathcal{F}_{s}]=R_{s}=1and Jensen’s inequality:𝔼μ​[(RT+1)2|ℱs]=𝔼μ​[RT+2​RT+1|ℱs]≤2+2​𝔼μ​[RT|ℱs]=4.\mathbb{E}_{\mu}\Big[\big(\sqrt{R_{T}}+1\big)^{2}\Big|\mathcal{F}_{s}\Big]=\mathbb{E}_{\mu}\Big[R_{T}+2\sqrt{R_{T}}+1\Big|\mathcal{F}_{s}\Big]\leq 2+2\sqrt{\mathbb{E}_{\mu}\big[R_{T}\big|\mathcal{F}_{s}\big]}=4.(B.26)

Concerning the first factor, we use the elementary inequality(x−1)2≤−ln⁡x+x−1(\sqrt{x}-1)^{2}\leq-\ln x+x-1, forx>0x>0, and recall that𝔼μ​[RT|ℱs]=Rs=1\mathbb{E}_{\mu}[R_{T}|\mathcal{F}_{s}]=R_{s}=1:𝔼μ​[(RT−1)2|ℱs]≤𝔼μ​[−ln⁡RT+RT−1|ℱs]=−𝔼μ​[ln⁡RT|ℱs].\mathbb{E}_{\mu}\Big[\big(\sqrt{R_{T}}-1\big)^{2}\Big|\mathcal{F}_{s}\Big]\leq\mathbb{E}_{\mu}\big[-\ln R_{T}+R_{T}-1\big|\mathcal{F}_{s}\big]=-\mathbb{E}_{\mu}\big[\ln R_{T}\big|\mathcal{F}_{s}\big].(B.27)

Recall the definition ofRTR_{T}in (B.16) and use the martingale property for((μ−ν)∙Wμ)\big((\mu-\nu)\mathbin{\vbox{\hbox{\tiny$\bullet$}}}W^{\mu}\big), underℚμ\mathbb{Q}^{\mu}, to compute its logarithm:−𝔼μ​[ln⁡RT|ℱs]=12​𝔼μ​[∫0T|μr−νr|2​dr|ℱs].-\mathbb{E}_{\mu}\big[\ln R_{T}\big|\mathcal{F}_{s}\big]=\frac{1}{2}\mathbb{E}_{\mu}\bigg[\int_{0}^{T}|\mu_{r}-\nu_{r}|^{2}\,\mathrm{d}r\bigg|\mathcal{F}_{s}\bigg].(B.28)

Recall thatν=μ\nu=\muonΩ×[0,s]{\Omega}\times[0,s], then reinsert (B.27) and (B.26) into (B.25) and finally (B.25) into (B.23) to reach the conclusion.

Proof of(iii).Suppose now thatν,μ∈BMOT\nu,\mu\in\mathrm{BMO}_{T}satisfyν=μ\nu=\muonΩ×(s,a){\Omega}\times(s,a), for some0≤s<a≤T0\leq s<a\leq T.
ThenRs=RaR_{s}=R_{a}.
As a first step, suppose thatXXisℱa\mathcal{F}_{a}-measurable, real-valued, and integrable with respect to bothℚν\mathbb{Q}^{\nu}andℚμ\mathbb{Q}^{\mu}, then𝔼ν​[X|ℱs]=𝔼μ​[RT​X|ℱs]​Rs−1=𝔼μ​[𝔼μ​[RT​X|ℱa]|ℱs]​Rs−1.\mathbb{E}_{\nu}[X|\mathcal{F}_{s}]=\mathbb{E}_{\mu}[R_{T}X|\mathcal{F}_{s}]R_{s}^{-1}=\mathbb{E}_{\mu}\big[\mathbb{E}_{\mu}[R_{T}X|\mathcal{F}_{a}]\big|\mathcal{F}_{s}\big]R_{s}^{-1}.

SinceXXisℱa\mathcal{F}_{a}-measurable andRRis aℚμ\mathbb{Q}^{\mu}-martingale, we have𝔼μ​[RT​X|ℱa]=X​𝔼μ​[RT|ℱa]=X​Ra\mathbb{E}_{\mu}[R_{T}X|\mathcal{F}_{a}]=X\mathbb{E}_{\mu}[R_{T}|\mathcal{F}_{a}]=XR_{a}.
Therefore, we reach the claim for an integrable random variable𝔼ν​[X|ℱs]=𝔼μ​[X​Ra|ℱs]​Rs−1=𝔼μ​[X​Ra​Rs−1|ℱs]=𝔼μ​[X|ℱs].\mathbb{E}_{\nu}[X|\mathcal{F}_{s}]=\mathbb{E}_{\mu}[XR_{a}|\mathcal{F}_{s}]R_{s}^{-1}=\mathbb{E}_{\mu}\big[XR_{a}R_{s}^{-1}\big|\mathcal{F}_{s}\big]=\mathbb{E}_{\mu}[X|\mathcal{F}_{s}].

Assume now thatXXisℱa\mathcal{F}_{a}-measurable and[0,+∞][0,+\infty]-valued.
For alln∈ℕn\in\mathbb{N}, define the bounded (hence integrable) random variableXn:=X∧nX_{n}:=X\wedge n.
By the previous step we have𝔼ν​[Xn|ℱs]=𝔼μ​[Xn|ℱs]\mathbb{E}_{\nu}[X_{n}|\mathcal{F}_{s}]=\mathbb{E}_{\mu}[X_{n}|\mathcal{F}_{s}].
By the conditional monotone convergence theorem, we reach𝔼μ​[X|ℱs]:=limn→∞𝔼μ​[Xn|ℱs]=limn→∞𝔼ν​[Xn|ℱs]=𝔼ν​[X|ℱs],\mathbb{E}_{\mu}[X|\mathcal{F}_{s}]:=\lim_{n\to\infty}\mathbb{E}_{\mu}[X_{n}|\mathcal{F}_{s}]=\lim_{n\to\infty}\mathbb{E}_{\nu}[X_{n}|\mathcal{F}_{s}]=\mathbb{E}_{\nu}[X|\mathcal{F}_{s}],

the equality and the limits holdingℙ\mathbb{P}-a.s. as[0,+∞][0,+\infty]-valued random variables.

Eventually, assume thatXXisℱa\mathcal{F}_{a}-measurable and[−∞,+∞)[-\infty,+\infty)-valued such thatX+X^{+}is integrable with respect to bothℚμ\mathbb{Q}^{\mu}andℚν\mathbb{Q}^{\nu}.
Then the first step gives𝔼ν​[X+|ℱs]=𝔼μ​[X+|ℱs]\mathbb{E}_{\nu}[X^{+}|\mathcal{F}_{s}]=\mathbb{E}_{\mu}[X^{+}|\mathcal{F}_{s}], while the second gives𝔼ν​[X−|ℱs]=𝔼μ​[X−|ℱs]\mathbb{E}_{\nu}[X^{-}|\mathcal{F}_{s}]=\mathbb{E}_{\mu}[X^{-}|\mathcal{F}_{s}], as[0,+∞][0,+\infty]-valued random variables.
Subtracting the conditional expectations of the positive and negative parts, we reach𝔼μ[X|ℱs]:=𝔼μ[X+|ℱs]−𝔼μ[X−|ℱs]=𝔼ν[X+|ℱs]−𝔼ν[X−|ℱs]=:𝔼ν[X|ℱs],\mathbb{E}_{\mu}[X|\mathcal{F}_{s}]:=\mathbb{E}_{\mu}[X^{+}|\mathcal{F}_{s}]-\mathbb{E}_{\mu}[X^{-}|\mathcal{F}_{s}]=\mathbb{E}_{\nu}[X^{+}|\mathcal{F}_{s}]-\mathbb{E}_{\nu}[X^{-}|\mathcal{F}_{s}]=:\mathbb{E}_{\nu}[X|\mathcal{F}_{s}],

hence the equality between the[−∞,+∞)[-\infty,+\infty)-valued conditional expectations.□\Box

## Proof of Lemma22.

As in the proof of Lemma11, letA∈𝒫⊂ℱ⊗ℬ​([0,T])A\in\mathcal{P}\subset\mathcal{F}\otimes\mathscr{B}([0,T])withℙ⊗ℓ1​(A)=0\mathbb{P}\otimes\ell_{1}(A)=0be such that the properties listed in(𝐂𝐋)\mathbf{(C_{L})}or(𝐂𝐐)\mathbf{(C_{Q})}, as well as the conclusions of
Lemma11, hold onAc:=(Ω×[0,T])∖AA^{c}:=(\Omega\times[0,T])\setminus A.

We first define a version ofggby settingg~​(ω,r,z):={g​(ω,r,z),if​(ω,r,z)∈Ac×ℝm,0,if​(ω,r,z)∈A×ℝm.\widetilde{g}(\omega,r,z):=\begin{cases}g(\omega,r,z),&\text{if }(\omega,r,z)\in A^{c}\times\mathbb{R}^{m},\\
0,&\text{if }(\omega,r,z)\in A\times\mathbb{R}^{m}.\end{cases}

For every fixedz∈ℝmz\in\mathbb{R}^{m}, the map(ω,r)↦g~​(ω,r,z)(\omega,r)\mapsto\widetilde{g}(\omega,r,z)is predictable.
Moreover, for every(ω,r)(\omega,r), the mapz↦g~​(ω,r,z)z\mapsto\widetilde{g}(\omega,r,z)is finite and continuous onℝm\mathbb{R}^{m}.
Henceg~\widetilde{g}is a finite Carathéodory integrand, and therefore a normal integrand by[Rockafellar+Wets_1998_Variational_analysis, Example 14.29].
Consequently,[Rockafellar+Wets_1998_Variational_analysis, Theorem 14.56]applied tog~\widetilde{g}and to thepredictable map(ω,r)↦0∈ℝm(\omega,r)\mapsto 0\in\mathbb{R}^{m}yields thatK:Ω×[0,T]∋(ω,r)↦∂zg~​(ω,r,0)⊆ℝmK:{\Omega}\times[0,T]\ni(\omega,r)\mapsto\partial_{z}\widetilde{g}(\omega,r,0)\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{m}

is a closed-valued𝒫\mathcal{P}-measurable multifunction in the sense of[Rockafellar+Wets_1998_Variational_analysis, Definition 14.1].
Now define the support function ofKKbyh​(ω,r,z):=supq∈K​(ω,r)q⋅z={supq∈∂zg​(ω,r,0)q⋅z,if​(ω,r,z)∈Ac×ℝm,0,if​(ω,r,z)∈A×ℝm,h(\omega,r,z):=\sup_{q\in K(\omega,r)}q\cdot z=\begin{cases}\displaystyle\sup_{q\in\partial_{z}g(\omega,r,0)}q\cdot z,&\text{if }(\omega,r,z)\in A^{c}\times\mathbb{R}^{m},\\
0,&\text{if }(\omega,r,z)\in A\times\mathbb{R}^{m},\end{cases}

which is a convex normal integrand by[Rockafellar+Wets_1998_Variational_analysis, Example 14.51].
In particular,hhis𝒫⊗ℬ​(ℝm)\mathcal{P}\otimes\mathscr{B}(\mathbb{R}^{m})-measurable by[Rockafellar+Wets_1998_Variational_analysis, Corollary 14.34].

We now prove the statements(i),(iii)onAcA^{c}.
Fix henceforth(ω,r)∈Ac(\omega,r)\in A^{c}.

The statement(i)is satisfied by construction.

Sincez↦h​(ω,r,z)z\mapsto h(\omega,r,z)is a support function, it is sublinear by[Rockafellar+Wets_1998_Variational_analysis, Theorem 8.24].
Forq∈∂zg​(ω,r,0)q\in\partial_{z}g({\omega},r,0)andz,z′∈ℝmz,z^{\prime}\in\mathbb{R}^{m}we haveq⋅z≤q⋅z′+q⋅(z−z′)≤q⋅z′+R​|z−z′|,q\cdot z\leq q\cdot z^{\prime}+q\cdot(z-z^{\prime})\leq q\cdot z^{\prime}+R|z-z^{\prime}|,

whereR:=LR:=Lifggsatisfies(𝐂𝐋)\mathbf{(C_{L})}andR:=2​kR:=2kifggsatisfies(𝐂𝐐)\mathbf{(C_{Q})}, thanks to Lemma11(i)(i)(c).
Take the supremum overqqto haveh​(ω,r,z)−h​(ω,r,z′)≤R​|z−z′|h({\omega},r,z)-h({\omega},r,z^{\prime})\leq R|z-z^{\prime}|.
Exchangingzzandz′z^{\prime}yields|h​(ω,r,z)−h​(ω,r,z′)|≤R​|z−z′||h({\omega},r,z)-h({\omega},r,z^{\prime})|\leq R|z-z^{\prime}|, that is the Lipschitz continuity with constantRRforz↦h​(ω,r,z)z\mapsto h({\omega},r,z).
This shows(iii).

It remains to prove(ii).
Consider the product spaceE:=Ω×[0,T]×ℝmE:=\Omega\times[0,T]\times\mathbb{R}^{m}endowed with theσ\sigma-algebra𝒫⊗ℬ​(ℝm)\mathcal{P}\otimes\mathscr{B}(\mathbb{R}^{m}), and define the multifunctionK¯:E∋(ω,r,z)↦K​(ω,r)⊆ℝm\overline{K}:E\ni(\omega,r,z)\mapsto K(\omega,r)\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{m}.
The multifunctionK¯\overline{K}is closed-valued by Lemma11(i)(i)(c)and is𝒫⊗ℬ​(ℝm)\mathcal{P}\otimes\mathscr{B}(\mathbb{R}^{m})-measurable becauseK¯−1​(O)=K−1​(O)×ℝm∈𝒫⊗ℬ​(ℝm)\overline{K}^{-1}(O)=K^{-1}(O)\times\mathbb{R}^{m}\in\mathcal{P}\otimes\mathscr{B}(\mathbb{R}^{m})for every openO⊆ℝmO\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{m}.
Then the functionF:E×ℝm∋((ω,r,z),q)⟼{−q⋅z,if​q∈K¯​(ω,r,z),+∞,if​q∉K¯​(ω,r,z),F:E\times\mathbb{R}^{m}\ni(({\omega},r,z),q)\longmapsto\begin{cases}-q\cdot z,&\text{if }q\in\overline{K}({\omega},r,z),\\
+\infty,&\text{if }q\notin\overline{K}({\omega},r,z),\\
\end{cases}

is a normal integrand by[Rockafellar+Wets_1998_Variational_analysis, Example 14.32].
By[Rockafellar+Wets_1998_Variational_analysis, Theorem 14.37], the argmin multifunctionΓ:E∋(ω,r,z)⟼arg​minq∈ℝm⁡F​((ω,r,z),q)=arg​maxq∈K​(ω,r)⁡q⋅z\Gamma:E\ni(\omega,r,z)\longmapsto\operatorname*{arg\,min}_{q\in\mathbb{R}^{m}}F((\omega,r,z),q)=\operatorname*{arg\,max}_{q\in K({\omega},r)}q\cdot z

is closed-valued and𝒫⊗ℬ​(ℝm)\mathcal{P}\otimes\mathscr{B}(\mathbb{R}^{m})-measurable.
In additionΓ​(ω,r,z)\Gamma({\omega},r,z)is nonempty for any(ω,r,z)∈E({\omega},r,z)\in Ebecauseq↦q⋅zq\mapsto q\cdot zis continuous andK​(ω,r)K({\omega},r)is compact by Lemma11(i)(i)(c).
Therefore, the measurable selection theorem[Rockafellar+Wets_1998_Variational_analysis, Corollary 14.6]yields a𝒫⊗ℬ​(ℝm)\mathcal{P}\otimes\mathscr{B}(\mathbb{R}^{m})-measurable mapμ¯:Ω×[0,T]×ℝm→ℝm\bar{\mu}:\Omega\times[0,T]\times\mathbb{R}^{m}\to\mathbb{R}^{m}such thatμ¯∈Γ\bar{\mu}\in\Gammaeverywhere inEE, namelyμ¯​(ω,r,z)∈K​(ω,r)andμ¯​(ω,r,z)⋅z=maxq∈K​(ω,r)⁡q⋅z=h​(ω,r,z),∀(ω,r,z)∈E.\bar{\mu}(\omega,r,z)\in K(\omega,r)\quad\text{and}\quad\bar{\mu}(\omega,r,z)\cdot z=\max_{q\in K(\omega,r)}q\cdot z=h(\omega,r,z),\qquad\forall\,({\omega},r,z)\in E.

Then(ii)holds onAcA^{c}.□\Box

Then the condition(Hs,t)is satisfied for every0≤s≤t<T0\leq s\leq t<T.

## Proof.

DefineΦ:[0,+∞)→[0,+∞)\Phi:[0,+\infty)\to[0,+\infty)such thatΦ​(x)=maxq∈Γ​(x)⁡d2​(q)\Phi(x)=\max_{q\in\Gamma(x)}d^{2}(q), whereΓ​(x):={q∈ℝm:g∗​(q)≤x}\Gamma(x):=\{q\in\mathbb{R}^{m}\ :\ g^{\ast}(q)\leq x\}is the sublevel set ofg∗g^{\ast}at levelxxandd:ℝm∋q↦dist⁡(q,∂g​(0))d:\mathbb{R}^{m}\ni q\mapsto\operatorname{dist}(q,\partial g(0)).

Indeed, the sublevels sets ofg∗g^{\ast}are closed becauseg∗g^{\ast}is lower semi-continuous by Lemma11(i)(i)(a), and are bounded thanks to the two items in Lemma11(i)(i)(a).

The first inequality above follows fromq∈Γ​(g∗​(q))q\in\Gamma(g^{\ast}(q))ifg∗​(q)g^{\ast}(q)is finite, otherwise setΦ​(+∞)=ψ​(+∞)=+∞\Phi(+\infty)=\psi(+\infty)=+\infty.
It remains to prove thatlim infx→0+ψ​(x)=0\liminf_{x\to 0^{+}}\psi(x)=0.

First note thatΦ​(x)→0\Phi(x)\to 0asx→0+x\to 0^{+}.
Indeed, if not, there existη>0\eta>0,xn→0+x_{n}\to 0^{+}andqn∈Γ​(xn)q_{n}\in\Gamma(x_{n})such thatd2​(qn)≥ηd^{2}(q_{n})\geq\eta.
Since(xn)n∈ℕ(x_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}is bounded and the sublevel sets ofg∗g^{\ast}are compact, there existsq∈ℝmq\in\mathbb{R}^{m}and a subsequenceqnk→qq_{n_{k}}\to q.
By lower semi-continuity,g∗​(q)≤lim infk→∞g∗​(qnk)≤lim infk→∞xnk=0.g^{\ast}(q)\leq\liminf_{k\to\infty}g^{\ast}(q_{n_{k}})\leq\liminf_{k\to\infty}x_{n_{k}}=0.

Sinceg∗≥0g^{\ast}\geq 0, we getg∗​(q)=0g^{\ast}(q)=0. Henceq∈∂g​(0)q\in\partial g(0), and therefored​(qnk)→d​(q)=0d(q_{n_{k}})\to d(q)=0, a contradiction.

Moreover, by the two items in Lemma11(i)(i)(a), there existA,B≥0A,B\geq 0such thatΦ​(x)≤A+B​x\Phi(x)\leq A+Bxforx≥0x\geq 0(chooseA=L2,B=0A=L^{2},B=0under(𝐂𝐋)\mathbf{(C_{L})}andA=4​k2,B=4​kA=4k^{2},B=4kunder(𝐂𝐐)\mathbf{(C_{Q})}).

Fix nowε>0{\varepsilon}>0.
SinceΦ​(x)→0\Phi(x)\to 0asx→0+x\to 0^{+}, chooseδ>0\delta>0such thatΦ​(x)≤ε\Phi(x)\leq{\varepsilon}for0≤x≤δ0\leq x\leq\delta.
Then, forx>δx>\delta, chooseMε>0M_{\varepsilon}>0large enough (e.g.,Mε=B+A/δM_{\varepsilon}=B+A/\delta) such thatΦ​(x)≤A+B​x≤Mε​x\Phi(x)\leq A+Bx\leq M_{\varepsilon}x.
Therefore, we have−ε−Mε​x≤−Φ​(x)-{\varepsilon}-M_{\varepsilon}x\leq-\Phi(x)for allx≥0x\geq 0.
The map[0,+∞)∋x↦−ε−Mε​x[0,+\infty)\ni x\mapsto-{\varepsilon}-M_{\varepsilon}xis convex and is less than or equal to−Φ-\Phi.
By definition offf, we infer−ε−Mε​x≤f​(x)≤−Φ​(x)≤0-{\varepsilon}-M_{\varepsilon}x\leq f(x)\leq-\Phi(x)\leq 0, thus0≤ψ​(x)=−f​(x)≤ε+Mε​x0\leq\psi(x)=-f(x)\leq{\varepsilon}+M_{\varepsilon}x.
Takingx→0+x\to 0^{+}and by arbitrariness ofε>0{\varepsilon}>0, we conclude thatlimx→0+ψ​(x)=0\lim_{x\to 0^{+}}\psi(x)=0.□\Box

## Appendix DProof of the main theorem in the Lipschitz case

## Proof of Theorem15under(a).

We here explain in detail how the proof of Theorem15under(b)can be adapted to the case(a), which allows for a more general choice of the inner risk processπ\piby restricting the class of convex risk measuresρ{\rho}(imposing stricter conditions on the driversgg).

Henceforth assume thatggsatisfies(𝐂𝐋)\mathbf{(C_{L})}, andb∈LT2b\in L^{2}_{T}.
Then the dual domainℬ⊆BMOT\mathcal{B}\subseteq\mathrm{BMO}_{T}for the induced risk measureρ{\rho}consists of predictable processesμ:Ω×[0,T]→ℝm\mu:{\Omega}\times[0,T]\to\mathbb{R}^{m}essentially bounded byLL, as stated in Proposition13.
In this setting, dealing with the dual representation forρ{\rho}is easier thanks to the uniform boundedness of the dual driftsμ∈ℬ\mu\in\mathcal{B}at all times.
Thus the main strategy of the proof that we presented in the quadratic case still holds in the Lipschitz case.
The only parts that need further analysis are the ones that were based on the uniform boundedness ofbbandσ{\sigma}.
We review, one by one, the steps of the previous proof, discussing how they need to be modified under the new assumptions.

Step 1is unchanged, with the assumptionsb,σ∈LT2b,{\sigma}\in L^{2}_{T}guaranteeing thatΔε​πt∈L2\Delta_{\varepsilon}\pi_{t}\in L^{2}.

Step 2also remains unchanged.
In order to apply Lemma20(iii), replace (3.66) by(∫tt+ε(br+σr⋅μ~r)​𝑑r−∫st+εg∗​(r,μ~r)​dr)+≤∫tt+ε(|br|+L​|σr|)​dr.\left(\int_{t}^{t+{\varepsilon}}(b_{r}+{\sigma}_{r}\cdot\tilde{\mu}_{r})\,dr-\int_{s}^{t+{\varepsilon}}g^{\ast}(r,\tilde{\mu}_{r})\,\mathrm{d}r\right)^{+}\leq\int_{t}^{t+{\varepsilon}}\big(|b_{r}|+L|{\sigma}_{r}|\big)\,\mathrm{d}r.

The right-hand side belongs toL2L^{2}, hence toL1​(ℚμ)∩L1​(ℚμ~)L^{1}(\mathbb{Q}^{\mu})\cap L^{1}(\mathbb{Q}^{\tilde{\mu}})by Lemma12(iii)(withK=LK=L,u=1u=1,p=2p=2).
Therefore the conclusion (3.67) of Step 2 remains valid.

Step 3maintains the same structure with the same set𝒞εs,t\mathcal{C}_{\varepsilon}^{s,t}, but the final estimate (3.74) needs changing becausebbandσ{\sigma}are no longer bounded processes.
In detail, from (3.68) and fromb+σ⋅μ−12​g∗​(⋅,μ)≤b+σ⋅μ≤|b|+L​|σ|,b+\sigma\cdot\mu-\frac{1}{2}g^{\ast}(\,\cdot\,,\mu)\leq b+\sigma\cdot\mu\leq|b|+L|\sigma|,

we inferVεs,t​(μ)≤𝔼μ​[1ε​∫tt+ε(|br|+L​|σr|)​dr|ℱs]−12​ε​Gt,t+εs​(μ)−1ε​Gs,t​(μ).V_{\varepsilon}^{s,t}(\mu)\leq\mathbb{E}_{\mu}\left[\left.\frac{1}{{\varepsilon}}\int_{t}^{t+{\varepsilon}}\big(|b_{r}|+L|\sigma_{r}|\big)\,\mathrm{d}r\right|\mathcal{F}_{s}\right]-\frac{1}{2{\varepsilon}}G^{s}_{t,t+{\varepsilon}}(\mu)-\frac{1}{{\varepsilon}}G_{s,t}(\mu).

whereϖ~​(ε):=(ψ​(ε)+ε)1/(2​v)\tilde{\varpi}({\varepsilon}):=(\psi(\sqrt{\varepsilon})+{\varepsilon})^{1/(2v)}satisfiesϖ~​(ε)→0\tilde{\varpi}({\varepsilon})\to 0asε→0+{\varepsilon}\to 0^{+}.

where in the last inequality we used(x+y)+≤x++y+(x+y)^{+}\leq x^{+}+y^{+}, forx,y∈ℝx,y\in\mathbb{R}.

By (D.13), (D.14) and the tower property, the family(Zε)ε>0(Z_{\varepsilon})_{{\varepsilon}>0}is uniformly bounded inL2L^{2}.
Thus, for everyδ>0\delta>0, Markov’s inequality gives, asε→0+{\varepsilon}\to 0^{+}:ℙ​((Uεs,t−14​ε)+>δ)≤ℙ​(Zε>14​ε+δ)≤𝔼​[Zε2](14​ε+δ)2⟶0.\mathbb{P}\left(\left(U_{\varepsilon}^{s,t}-\frac{1}{4\sqrt{\varepsilon}}\right)^{+}>\delta\right)\leq\mathbb{P}\left(Z_{\varepsilon}>\frac{1}{4\sqrt{\varepsilon}}+\delta\right)\leq\frac{\mathbb{E}[Z_{\varepsilon}^{2}]}{\left(\frac{1}{4\sqrt{\varepsilon}}+\delta\right)^{2}}\longrightarrow 0.

Thus, the first positive part converges to0in probability.
Moreover, (D.14) and the uniformL2L^{2}-bound onZεZ_{\varepsilon}show that it is uniformly integrable. Hence it converges to0inL1L^{1}by Vitali’s theorem.
The second positive part on the right-hand side of (D.12) converges to0ℙ\mathbb{P}-a.s. and is dominated by(−𝔼μ0​[Yt|ℱs])+∈L1\big(-\mathbb{E}_{\mu^{0}}[Y_{t}|\mathcal{F}_{s}]\big)^{+}\in L^{1}, thus it converges to0inL1L^{1}by dominated convergence.
Therefore, both terms on the right-hand side of (D.12) converge to0inL1L^{1}, leading to(ess​supμ∈ℬεs,t∖𝒞εs,t⁡Vεs,t​(μ)−Hs,t)+⟶0,in​L1.\left(\operatorname*{ess\,sup}_{\mu\in\mathcal{B}_{\varepsilon}^{s,t}\setminus\mathcal{C}_{\varepsilon}^{s,t}}V_{\varepsilon}^{s,t}(\mu)-H_{s,t}\right)^{+}\longrightarrow 0,\qquad\text{in }L^{1}.(D.15)

We can finally conclude as in the quadratic case.
Forttin the fullℓ1\ell_{1}-measure Borel set𝒯:=𝒯1∩𝒯~2\mathcal{T}:=\mathcal{T}_{1}\cap\widetilde{\mathcal{T}}_{2}, we can use the newly proved convergences (D.7), (D.8) and (D.15) in the old estimates (3.106) and (3.100), yielding (3.107).
The well-posedness of the resilience evaluation follows directly from the convergence of the positive and negative parts.
TheL2L^{2}-bound (3.43) easily follows from the definition ofHs,tH_{s,t}, Lemma12(iii)and the linear growth bound forgg.□\Box
